MOTOR SMASH AT HELSBY. Lieutenant Ireland-Blackburne Injured.

Lieutenant Gilbert Treland-Blackburne,son of Colonel Ireland-Blackburne, C. 8.. of Hale Hall, Hale, near Widnes, and his chauffeur, named Sothern, were seriously injured in a motor smash at Helsby, Cheshire, on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. Blackburne left home to motor to Chester, in company with Captain and Mrs. Hulme, Miss Fluke, and the chauffeur. Just outside Helsby, going down a steep hill, the motor skidded on the greasy road, a wheel came off, and the vehicle completely overturned. All the party were thrown out violently. Lieutenant Blackburne, who was driving the car at the time of the accident, sustained a bad fracture to the thigh. The chauffeur, Sothern, had his face badly cut. Captain Hulme and the ladies escaped practically unhurt, though suffering very much from shock. The injured men were conveyed to Chester Infirmary, after having been attended by Dr. Briant, of Helsby. Later, Lieutenant Blackburne was removed to his home. .

Lieutenant Blackburne's coming of age last week was the occasion of great festivities at Hale Hall, and the many handsome presentations made to him included from his father the motor car to which the accident happened.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0007.txt | included=true | included =====
FURTHER DETAILS.

Interviewing idr:- Aii;;; a VVlCine—s Contractor. who I was first on the scene after the accident, a Widnes representative was informed that Mr. Blackburne was driving only about fourteen miles an hour, Mr. Allen's car stopped to let Mr. Blackburne's car pass, and no sooner had it done so, and Mr. Allen was turning his car round, than he saw the other car run up the cop after skidding. Every pain of glass in the car was broken,and the ladies and Captain Hulme were got out at the top. Attention was at once called to Mr. Blackburne, and when it was found that his leg was broken two pieces of wood were pulled from the edges. Motor-veils and handkerchiefs were used by the doctor and witness to place the limb into splints. The ambulance was sent for from Chester, but it was not needed as the ambulance from Helsby works was used to convey him to the Horse and Jockey Hotel. After a consultation it was decided to take him home; so after carrying him a mile to the station be WWI sent to Ditton Station, where the Widnes horse ambulance conveyed him home to Hale Hall. The ladies behaved splendidly, and Captain Hulme proved a great source of comfort to the young gentleman in his misfortune.

"ONLY THREE WEEKS TO LIVE."

CHAS. FORCES Bile Beans cured her.

DIDIAITION't CONSTIPATION', & SIIEUXATISIL.

"The last doctor I had told me I had only three weeks to live But Chas. Forde's original Bile Beans cured me more than four years ago, and I have since remained in splendid health." This is the declaration of Mrs. Hallwood, of 48, Edith-road, Seacombe, Cheshire. Mrs. Hallwood continued :—" I had been a Jufferer from biliousness from childhood, fully thirty years. WINTER FOOD.

Winter Food should be such as will supply the body with enough animal heit to resist the effects of weather temperature, etc. Currants are two-thirds their weight natural sugar, the element which contributes heat to the human organism.

Currants are fresh grapes of a seedless variety, grown only in Greece and dried in the open air by exposure to the sun. The practice of drying fruits is almost as old as tide hills. It, renders food economic, and increases their nstritive properties. A pound of sun-dried Currants contains all the nutriment of flve pounds of fresh grapes.

TO KEEP IVORY WHITE

Ivory knife handles should never be placed in water, for it discolours them; but even with the greatest care ivory will get yellow with use. To restore its whiteness as much as possible, ninke a •cream-like paste with powdere.r! whitenkur and spirits of wine. Rub this well on the handles and they will be cleaned and polished in the process.

BREATHING AND BEAUTY.

Now woman can have bright eyes, a beautiful skin, or an elastic step if she does not supply her lungs with oxygen. She can do this by deep breathing. The indolent woman regains her lost energy when she learns how to breathe correctly. The serum girl, with the dark circles under her eyes, discovers that with correct breathing the congested veins will yield to the stimulated cir- culation, the dark rings disappear, and the lustre reappears in the eyes. Deep breathing will help to make curves where there are angles, and it is a potent factor in the cure of emaciation, be- cause it supplies oxygen to the wasted tissues, and sets the machinery of the vital organs going.

WALNUT HAIR STAIN.

Fill a large jar rather loosely with finely. chopped husks. Pour in methylated spirit to cover the husks. Allow to remainikone week, closely stoppered or corked. Now pour off the liquid. Turn out the husks. Squeeze from them all the liquid obtainable. Mix the liquids, and keep in a well-corked bottle. Next, boil the husks in sufficient water; it should just cover them. Strain. Add the water to the spirit. This is a fairly good stain. It may be made much i stronger n the following way: After boiling for half-an-hour, strain, and boil down until it be- comes thickish. Then mix with the spirit pre- parations. Walnut stain is quite useless unless the hair has been previously freed from all grease.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
PARSLEY AND ITS USES.

Fresh parsley can never be improved on for imparting a delicate flavour to all kinds of savoury dishes, but in winter it is often difficult, to procure it, and the careful housewife never fails while parsley is plentiful to lay by a store for use later in tbe year. It must be freshly picked, and should be immersed for a few minutes in boiling water, then strained and placed on a large dish in a moderately cool oven. After it is quite dry it should be packed in air-tight jars and tins, and put away in the store cupboard till the winter. When required for flavouring, if it is soaked for a few moments in tepid water it will freshen it considerably. As a seasoning for soups and stews, dried parsley is without a rival, while finely chopped it can be used for garnish- ing cold joints and poultry, fricassees, and fried fish. The green juice pressed from fresh parsley is invaluable az. an innocuous colouring matter for savoury sauces, soups, or jellies.

" DON'TS " FOR HOUSEWIVES.

The following "don'ts" have been compiled for the woman who uses a gas stove:

Don't keep any kettle or cooking utensils over a one-jet simmering flame in exactly the same spot too long. The simmering flame is like the goldsmith's flame, boring, boring into the pots and vessels.

Don't forget, if you need hot water for sudden sickness in the night, that four quarts of water over as many burners will heat enough faster than four quarts in one vessel to more than make up for the extra flames.

Don't forget, if you come across triangular. shaped saucepans, to buy them, as several can be placed over one ring and kept boiling without any waste of gas.

- -- Don't forget it saves gas if when a roast of any kind is prepared for dinner it is arranged to have most of the other food cooked in the oven.. Have baked macaroni, or cauliflower au mratin, instead of a boiled vegetable. A baked pudding instead of a boiled one will save gas on these days.

WHAT TO EAT.

If people would study the various vegetables. fruits. cereals. &c., as to their medicinal ,quali- ties in adjusting ailments of the human body, they would rarely resort to such methods for relief as taking pills, paregoric, or patent medicines.

liy using discretion in selecting and combin- ing foods, and individualising as the case might be. far better results may be obtained. Some foods disagree 'with some persons, yet they continue tip impose on the already outraged stomach by eating the foods that nature in- tended for someone else, or for them at a later date possibly. Fruits and acid vegetables (tomatoes) are good for the liver. Lettuce, celery, dandelions, crosses, and simi- lar salads are good nerve tonics. Beans, peas, lentils. Stc., produce strength and heat, and are a good substitute for meats in winter.

_ In potatoes is too much starch for fat people. Asparagus and carrots are good for the Icidni.vs. Walnuts, almonds, and Spanish nuts are full of oils and nutriment. Are much more sustain- ing than meats and easily digested if thoroughly masticated.

Onions and radishes are preventives of colds and sore throat. Olive oil is much healthier than meat fats for cooking, is easier on digestive organs if you get it pure. To take a spoonful after a meal is recommended. In California, it is said, invalids drink olive oil a cupful at a time. It aids digestion, throat and lung trouble vanish, and thin people grow fat on a diet of fruits, nuts, and olive oil. _

Eggs are easier to digest when slightly boiled than when eaten raw. Macaroni and vermicelli, on account of their closeness of grain, are not as easily digested as light bread. Potatoes when mealy are easily digested. When close and watery they are hard to digest. Bananas, on account of the amount of nitro- gen they contain, are easily ingested. The sufferer from dyspepsia should find tur- nips, spinach, cresses, salads, celery, lettuce, dandelions, ripe peaches, and roasted apples on his bill of fare.

A person suffering from chronic rheumatism should avoid dried fish, pork, veal, and tr.:key.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Princes in the Navy.—Prince Edward of Wales has returned to Osborne College to resume his naval studies, but Prince Alexander of Fiattenberg, the eldest son of Princess Henry, has retired from the active list of the Navy. It is the unwritten law in the Royal Family that Princes shall be attached to one Service or the other, but Princess Henry has always wished that none of her sons should serve either in the Army or Navy, the reason being the untimely death of their father from fever contracted whilst on active service in Ashanti. Prince Louis of Battenberg, a nephew by marriage of Princess Henry, is the only prince now serving in the Navy. He has been in the Royal Navy for close upon forty years and is vice-admiral in the Mediterranean. His wife;Princess Louis, is the eldest daughter of the late Princess Alice. and their eldest child, named after her grandmother, Princess Alice, was married some four or five years ago, to Prince Andrew of Greece.

The Maryburgh Sanatorium.—That was a heart- warming incident which took place at Maryburgh, where the Seaforth Sanatoaium for consumptive patients was opened by Mrs. Stewart-Mackenzie, the lady whose munificent generosity has provided for the institution, and she herself addressed in a graceful speech the Highland people whom she loves. Mrs. Stewart-Mackenzie is a member of the family who raised both battalions of that splendid regiment the Seaforth Highlanders, and she remarked very happily that she was glad to be engaged in the work of arming and providing against an enemy more insidious, and much more deadly, than any foreign battalions. That is a subject upon which much could be said, for even in the army, which has on the aggregate a low death-rate, suffers more from disease than the bullets of the enemy. Fighting Consumption.—lt will be remembered how, during the campaign in South Africa, typhoid and dysentery caused terrible ravages among the troops, and nearly every regiment has a tradition of heavy mortality at one time or another from a similar cause. Perhaps the most remarkable of these stories is the well- attested case of the old 31st regiment, who in 1796 marched into St. Lucia 913 strong, aed seven months later, marched out with a total roll of 74. Such incidents as these are striking, and appeal to the imagination, but consumption is at work 11 the time and the aggregate mortality which it occasions is still more appalling. For this reason it is impossible to speak too highly of the generosity the public spirit and humanity which have impelled Mrs. Stewart. Mackenzie to make this princely gift to Rossline, and one is glad to know that the occasion elicited a sympathetic telegram from the King, whose interest in the provision of Sanatoria for consumptive patients, has been manifested on many occasions. Birds as Death Omens.—Believers in death omens regard the sudden fall of a picture as particularly signi- ficant, and there are a great many people who look with equal dread upon the appearance of a bird against the window pane, or in the house. Numerous correspon- dents have written to a London newspaper, giving instances where a death has occurred in the family soon after the appearance of a feathered visitor. The magpie is notoriously an unlucky bird, but it seems that the robin is most often the bird of ill-omen. That however is not surprising, because it is a very common thing in the winter time for robins to enter houses in the country. The robin is a pugnacious little fellow, and less afraid of man than many of his kind. When food is scarce he will come fearlessly within a foot or two of anyone digging the ground, on the look out for grubs and worms, and will not hesitate to go into the kitchen or pantry if an opportune occasion presents itself. There are few country houses that do not have any of these little visitors in the winter, and although ' coincidences occur, and are certain to recur, it is foolish' to attach any sinister significance to their presence. I A Suffragist Problem.—The Scottish woman graduates claim that according to statutory law, they are entitled to the Parliamentary vote for the Universities, on the ground that the Act enfranchising Scottish graduates makes use of the word "person "and not" man."—The Court of Session has on two occasions decided against them, but the graduates through their organisation, have now decided to appeal to tke House of Lords and it will be interesting to see how that, the highest legal authority, will deal with the matter,—and what solution it will suggest for the problem. When is a woman not a " person. —The judges have made little difficulty over it, hut sometimes judge-made law is one thing,and the statutory law another, and it is the latter only which concerns the House of Lords. The Franco-Brit:Bh Exhibition.—One of the twenty buildings at the Franco-British Exhibition, which is to be the great London attraction this year, is to be called the Palace of Women's Work. The Countess of Jersey is the President, and the Duchess of Sutherland Vice President of this Section, which is intended to be as complete an exhibition as possible of the arts and crafts of women. This will be interesting, although such an arrangement is apt to suggest that women's work alone is rather insignificant, and separately dis- played it does not do justice to the share performed by women in the arts industries, and manufactures. The main features of the Exhibition will he two large boulevards upon which are built twenty huge places, one of the boulevards representing Britain, and her colonies, and the other France and her colonies. The Stadium, in which the Olympian games and competi- tions are to take place, is to accommodate no fewer' than 80,000 people at one time. Dress at the Early Courts.—The show-rooms of lead- ing Court dressmakers, are just now thronged with customers selecting, and being fitted into gowns destined to appear nt one or the other of the early Courts. Generally speaking twenty guineas is the smallest sum for which a gown and train—the latter often costing more than the former—can be obtained, three to four hundrdd guineas being the most paid for such " crecilions." Three to five pounds a yard is frequently paid matrons for fabric for their trains, but for the rhibutante the simplest materials, such as chiffon, tulle, gauze, and net, are chosen, and the complete dress and train rarely exceeds £5O. Three yards is the regulation length for Court trains, but many girls and worsen indulge in the limit—four yards—the idea beingto turn the fabric contained in the train—after it has , swept past the King and Queen—into a dinner or ball dress.

An Old-World Fabric.—Nfoir4 antique will be muoh in evidence at the coming Courts, as is likely therefore to be exceedingly fashionable during the spring and summer. With its lustrous sheen which brings out to perfection the lovely tones of colour in which the moir4 is made, it is difficult to imagine how we could have I brought ourselves to give up so 'beau ti til a material, I but however that may be, we have certainly accorded it a warm welcome back. So far pale colours lead, such for instance, as pearl grey shot with rose and forget- me-not blue, the dark tones being set aside, at any rate, for the present. Little Luggage.—The experienced woman traveller has learned to reduce her luggage almost to vanishing point, and strange to say dressmakers, tailors, and milliners have done their best for her in this particular direction. There is, for example, the useful skirt the tailor has devised, made quite short and with a separare lining, upon which a flounce is arranged, which sweeps the ground. This underskirt can be added when occa- sions arise that make a long skirt desirable. Then there is the reversible skirt, one side plaid and the other plain, which is constructed on the same plan, and so that it can be worn either side. How to ring the necess- sary changes with raiment that takes up the mimimum of space, is almost a fine art with women travellers to-day, a welcome change from the times when no woman could be persuaded to take even a quite short holiday unless accompanied by a "Noah's Ark " which only the strongest could lift, a huge weighty and altogether useloss " dressing bag," curuberous " hold- alls," and various other impediments. DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

THE NEW DRAPED SKIRT. BY ALFRED TENNYSON, Draped skirts represent one of the promises of Author of "The Little Princess," "The Lath. La Mode for the coming summer, and the Yeti. Savage," "The Baronet," "A True Blue," "He ous and somewhat bizarre forms they are taking of the Stall," "Plutus on the Road," &e. at present point to the fact that dress will be not a little perplexing during °the coming months. The corselet skirt has by no means ceased to obtain its meed Of attention, being in many instances draped in close folds round the waist and over the -hips, a method which, the Erening Standard says, is somewhat inclined to impair the grace of the silhouette, while it is frequently surmounted by a wide band of heavy Venetian lace worked in gold thread, a long panel of the same running down the back and front of the gown to the hem. In cases where the skirt is fashioned in this manner the bodice is composed of closely-pleated dyed net' or lace over soft satin, with tight-fitting rucked sleeves finished with lace ruffles.

Mr. Abraham Hale, the cornchandler' was a tall, rather stout man, with sandy hair and reddish side-whiskers. His face was healthy and of a ruddy hue, and his somewhat prominent chin gave indications of obstinacy of character.

If you walked down one of the streets, or rather lanes, which led to the wharves at Lyne- mouth—narrow, cobbled streets they were, with old-fashioned, gabled, bow-windowed houses on either side—you passed Mr. Hale's house. The shop windows looked out on the street, and if (as it was possible for anyone of moderato stature to do) you looked through them you could see with some degree of pleasure the sanded floor, the shelves with bags of grain and meal neatly docketed, and perhaps behind the counter the agreeable, portly form of Mr. Hale himself, and his red figure-head gleaming rosily amidst the drab of canvas and deal with which he was surrounded. At the hack was a cosy parlour, with comfortable, chintze-covered chairs, tall grandfather's clock, ticking drowsily and sedately, jars of bright coloured flower's, and red window curtains, between which a prospect was obtained of the wharf, with its stone quays covered with coils of rope, planks, rusted anchors, and all the other paraphernalia of shipping. All day long one could see the jolly figures of mariners slowly consuming their pipes at the quay edge or gazing contemplatively at the decks of the vessels lying at the wharf. A tangle of spars and rigging made always a network of the blue, and the river swept out to sea beneath the eye, bearing upon its waters a continual variety of craft. It was in this pleasant room, with this prospect of all that made the life of Lynemouth, that Miss Dolly Hale the eornehandler's niece, held sway. Miss Hale was a young lady of con- siderable personal attractions, a fact which was generally recognised by the younger and more susceptible of the male population of Lynemouth ; but save for one trifling flirtation some two years ago, when she was only a girl, and which her uncle had sternly suppressed, she had shewn no inclination to make a choice amongst them, though her offers had doubtless been numerous.

A MODISH COIFFCRE.

Quite the lateet thing in hair-dressing is the modish evening coiffure pictured in our sketch. Though extremely smart in effect. this coiffure is not really difficult to manage if the hair be dressed over a small pad of well-waved natural hair. The chevelure is first waved all over in big careless-looking waves. The front

fi-c-f-- -- -,e4gw, Cil4i,gol±-,-, i 0,4 •aF,_ '''.,.--_-; tt ~a) lia w , / /

hair is then separated from the back and twisted into a loose knot in front to be out of the way. The back locks are divided into three or four pieces, each piece after being lightly "roughed" up at the back taken up to the crown of the head and there secured by a wee comb. The front hair is now combed over the face, the pad pinned into place, and the hair divided, taken over the pad, and secured with small combs on the crown of the head. All the hair at the top of the head is now tied together, and the ends arranged in a series of soft loose curls all over the crown of the head. ' The coiffure is corn- pleted by fixing into place the pretty simple fillet of gold or silver cord, which is finished on either side with loops and tasselled ends of the same cord.

Mr. Hale had selected a partner for her himself, but neither had she shewn any sympton of the tender passion for him—a fact hardly to be wondered at, as he was little younger than her relative, and decidedly plain in countenance. It is indeed to be feared that Mr. Hale encouraged the match from mercenary motives, as Sam Parrot was currently reported to be a warmish man and had a little property in the town, which made him a very respected member of society. Still, in spite of his niece's obstinacy, Mr. Hale continued to nourish a hope that she would at length see reason, and so made encouraging reports to his crony about his progress till that worthy, who was not conceited enough to have had very high hopes of success at first, began to Look in regularly of an evening "to have a pipe with Abraham Hale "after stimulating his courage at the Anchor. Though he had not been very keen on the thing originally, the honeyed words of his friend Mr. Hale and his infectious con- fidence had at length made him feel so certain of success that he actually began to like the prospect of married life in company with such a charming person as Miss Dolly Hale. When, therefore, all these hopes were dashed to the ground the shock to his vanity was rude. " 01iss Hale employed the last feminine resourca and burst into tears.

"I think you're very cruel and unkind." she

sobbed, "and I shall go upstairs to my bedroom at once "

As the door closed behind the graceful figure of his niece with a very pronounced bang, Mr. Hale admitted somewhat gloomily to himself that he had made a mess of it, a decision which he felt inclined to endorse more than once during the next two or three days.

A day or two after these events, as Mr. Hale was seated reading the paper behind the counter, he received a visit from a person who requested the favour of an interview. It was with an ominous foreboding that he conducted him into the room already described. The wind and the salt of the sea-air had covered the apple-like countenance of the visitor with a network of innumerable wrinkles, and flecked either cheek with a vivid splash of red, so that it had at once the littleness and the colour of a child's. A red muffler was wound round his throat, and his infantile body and limbs were encased in a suit of very rusty black. His legs were short and incredibly thin, and at the end of these somewhat farcical members stuck out at right angles a pair of feet of such prodigious size that it became a plizzle to know bow he managed to lift them when he had occasion to walk about.

Mr. Hale already knew Captain Latham, who had a small post at the port, by sight, and had perhaps met him two or three times in company, but their acquaintance was of the most casual. The conversation was, therefore, for some time of that kind when neither of the two persons engaged in it knows the other well, and yet is at the same time sure that there is an unpleasant subject to discuss before they part. What this was both men knew well, but so strange is human nature that they shewed the greatest unwillingness to approach it. With many tacks and nautical manceuvrings the captain beat 'up towards the goal, but no sooner did the reefs through which he must pass appear in sight than he would sheer off again to open sea. It was, however, he who at length mustered up courage to speak. "I don't know if you know my son, Mr. Hale ?" he said.

"I don't exactly recollect that I do," said the other, .inendagiously.

"He's a rare fine fellow," said Captain Latham, with enthusiasm, "and I'm proud to have him at home."

"Oh, he's been away?" said Mr. Hale, politely. "Yes, he's a sailor like his dad, and he's only just home from a two jrears' voyage in a tramp steamer. I expect he'll have a ship of his own before long," said Captain Latham, as enthu- siastically as before. Mr. Hale remarked that Captain Latham was fortunate to have such a son.

"Yes, he's always been a good boy to me," replied the old mariner, "and so I felt in duty bound to do what I could for him. "

"Very proper I'm sure," replied Mr. Hale, curtly, as he felt that the critical moment was approaching and that he must be on his guard. Captain Latham coughed and took a quick look at Mr. Hale.

The latter was obviously braced up to receive what was coining. His rosy face was rapidly becoming a rich purple, and his chin stuck out obstinately. The sailor was, however, quite as strongminded as his adversary, and was not to be frightened by these signs of coming storm. "I noticed," he continued, "that since his homecoming this time my son was a bit fidgety and restless, and I thought perhaps that he might have got into some trouble. I know that even the best go that way sometimes. So I asked him one day if there was anything on his mind; if there was, I would do my best to help him, whatever it might be. After a bit he told me that he had formed an attachment to a young woman of his acquaintance, and was anxious to marry her. I asked was his attachment returned. He said it was, but that the guardian of the young lady had forbidden him the house. Now, Mr. Hale, what I want to say is that my boy is a good boy, and fit to be a husband for any man's niece, and there is no reason why he should be prevented from marrying the girl of his choice." Mr. Hale preserved a diplomatic silence for a few moments after the captain finished speaking. Then withdrawing the pipe from his lips, he said slowly: "I'm sure I don't know why you should choose me to come to with this tale." "Because the girl in question is your niece," replied Captain Latham. "Well, you've come to the wrong box, that's all, Captain Latham," replied Mr. Hale. "When I say no I mean no, and I ain't going to altar my mind." "But surely it's worth considering a little? " replied the other. "I've done all the considering part already," said Mr. Hale, smacking his knee with the palm of his hand to emphasise his remarks. "I don't want some young oaf, only just in breeches, walking off with my girl. Give me a feller who knows his own mind and ain't above staying on shore and minding the shop." Captain Latham with difficulty kept his temper at this attack on his profession, but he was determined not to get angry. "And I don't think much of a young feller who daren't come here himself, but sends his father , to do all the talking." ~ _ .

"But I tell you tliat wasn't .the reason at all," replied the old sailor. "In the first place I offered to come here, and in the second place my son thought that two old chaps like you and me would have it out better."

"Well, he's wrong there," replied Mr. Hale; "it wouldn't make any difference if the King oi England was to come and ask me. When I say I won't have it I won't, and that's the long and short of it."

"Well, I suppose it's no good saying any more," said Captain Latham. "It ain't a bit of good," replied Mr. Hale, uncompromisingly. Twilight had approached while the two were wrangling, and it was almost dark when the little sailor, much incensed at the obstinacy and rudeness of Mr. Hale, took his leave. Through the parlour window, which was half open, Mr. Hale could see the lights being lit on the ships in the harbour.

The wharves became completely deserted as the various workmen finished their day's jobs and I trudged off to supper. It became practically dark in the parlour. Miss Hale still kept the ciignified seclusion of her bedroom, and in her absence the oil had run out, as Mr. Hale dis- covered when he endeavoured to light the lamp. He threw himself into the armchair with an expletive, and after lighting a pipe started to ponder over the events of the last few days. Whatever was the tenour of his reflections, he was obviously not meditating altering his previous standpoint; for as the dusk deepened in the room, and the evening mists began to creep over the river outside he exclaimed out loud, with an expression of which the tone and the language were alike reprehensible, that he would see himself in a very unpleasant situation before he consented to the marriage. At that moment he felt something hop quickly on to his knee. He looked down. A peculiar and frightful sensation came over him as he saw suddenly a pair of little red eyes gazing at him in the dusk. With a feeling of horror he observed that they belonged to a creature of dusky hue, with a long tail, which was seated on his knee. So great was the effect of the apparition that Mr. Hale sat rooted in his chair as if fixed there with iron clamps. He could not have moved hand or foot, and his scant and sandy locks seemed to rise up on his scalp. With a rapid movement the creature stood on its legs and pushed a black hand into the interstice between the buttons of Mr. Hale's shirt, thrusting for an instant its fiery eyes and grinning countenance into the petrified cornehandler's face. Then, so quickly that the eye could not fellow it, it vanished in the dusk. Mr. Hale still sat as if turned to stone.

"Good evening, Abraham," said Sam Parrot, who had just looked in for his evening smoke. "Why, whatever's the matter with you ?" he exclaimed, noticing the dejected attitude of his friend. "There's nothing the matter with me," answered Mr. Hale, shortly. "That's not true, Abraham,' replied Mr. Parrot. "I can see there's something wrong, simply by looking at you. You've been sitting here all in the dark."

"Well, I suppose I can sit in the dark if I like ?" said his friend, testily. "It costs nothing, anyhow." Mr. Parrot looked at his crony in amazement; this reception was so unlike his ordinary cheery greeting that ho felt sure there must he something CPriously the irrattrr. His emotions. however. were of a more definite cnaracter when the latter added, in a despondent tone: "And what's more, Sam Parrot, it's no good hanging about my girl. It's off!" - "Oh, it's off, is it?" said Parrot, in a choked

Voice, as his feelings nearly got the better ot him. "Hanging about, do you call it? Who was it suggested it, I should like to know? Who was it made me bring my pipe and 'baccer up here when I might have been sitting cosy in the parlour at the Anchor? Who was it made me dangle round a gal young enough to be my darter ? "

"It's no good you getting angry with me, Sam," interrupted Mr. Hale. "There's reasons or what I do."

"Bloomin' good reasons too, 1 expect!" rejoined Mr. Parrot, sarcastically. "Parrot, I've had a vision," said his friend, solemnly. "A what?" asked the other.

"A vision," replied Mr. Hale. "What, same as the prophets used to have in the Book ? "

"I reckon that's the size of it," said the other, gloomily. Mr. Parrot looked at his friend earnestly. The robust frame and temperament of Mr. Hale seemed so unlikely to have anything to do with any spirits except those which stood on the side- board that he drew an obvious inference. _

"Yu ain't been nipping, Abram have you?" Mr. Hale shook his head despondently. "Wish I had," he said; "but it ain't that." "Well, what is it?" said Sam Parrot, irritably. "What with your chopping and chang- ing and your visions and so forth, you ain't company for a plain man." "I can't tell you Sam; it was too terrible—set Inc all in a tremble. I feel I shall never get over it."

Mr. Parrot's curiosity was not proof against tuch allusions.

"Oh, let's have it, Abram Hale!" he cried.

Mr. hale gave a shudder, but after a moment be replied with an ashen face: "Sam, I've seen the devil I"

"Heaven bless me!" exclaimed Parrot, its. voluntarily drawing back. "Yes, I have," said his crony,with a rush now that his reserve concerning the incident was banished. With a pallid countenance and a hushed voice he told the story of what had occurred.

"What was he like?" asked Parrot,whose first feelings of awe had begun gradually to disappear, and were succeeded by considerable interest as to the appearance of our ancient enemy. "It was an awful little black imp," said Mr. Hale, with his teeth chattering; "and the first thing I noticed was him sitting on my knee with his eyes flashing red in the dark, and grinning up as maliciously as anything into my face. And whilst I was sitting there all in a maze he slipped his hand inside my shirt just over the helirt, and I felt the blood run cold in my veins." Mr. Hale buried his face in his hands and remained silent. When he lifted his head again it was to find Parrot, hat and stick in hand, pre- paring to leave the room. "You ain't going, Sam ?" he said. "I think I must," answered his friend, slowly.. "Don't go, Sam," cried Mr. Hale, wildly "Supposing if it was to come back ?" "That's just what I was thinking," said Mr. Parrot, maliciously. "Besides, it's you it wants to see, not me." _ . . .

_ "Sa,m !" said Mr. Hale, in an imploring voice. But his friend was already outside the door. He found time, however, to stick his head into the room again and observe with a grin: "Get your niece to take care of you, Abram."

_ , Mr. 'Hale remained where he was for *some time after his friend had gone. Then he walked half- way to the door, turned back to his armchair, and then moved towards the door again. Evidently he was in a state of indecision. As a matter of fact, the parting shaft of Mr. Parrot had stuck deep in his mind. Why not make it up with his niece ?"

"Dolly," he called at the foot of the stairs. There was silence above; he called again. "Yes, uncle," at length replied a voice. "Would you mind coming downstairs, I want to Ppeak to you." Mr. Hale's tones were so very submissive that Miss Halo realised at once, with a woman's instinct, that she had conquered. In another moment she appeared on the threshold. "Oh! Dolly," said Mr. Hale, "I wanted to say that—er----I've changed my mind, and that I see no objection to your asking young Latham to pay me a visit." -.Nfiss Hale did not abuse her victory, as she might have done. She threw her arms round her uncle's neck.

"I knew all the time you didn't mean it, dear," she whispered. "I'll write to Fred to- night."

The only sign that still shewed Mr. Hale's- tender point was that at the word "Fred" he gave a slight start.

The livery stable carriage, containing the married pair, had just driven off to the station, with a somewhat immature coachman on the box, bedecked with favours, and handling the ribbons with an air of ceremony which a naturally mobile countenance, and a strong inclination to grin, rather discounted. The respective fathers watched the bridal cortece depart with widely different emotions; but while one was naturally averse to revealing his chagrin, the other was as uunwilling to show a joy which might, consider- ing the circumstances of the case, have seemed indecent.

"Well, Mr. Hale," exclaimed Captain Latham at last, "won't you come in and drink their health ?"

It was a bitter blow to the pride of Abraham Hale to have to confess defeat, and it was still more bitter to have to enter in under his adver- sary's roof-tree in a spirit of reconciliation, but common sense told him that it was no good tc. refuse an invitation obviously offered in no unfriendly spirit. So with sufficiently good grace he remarked his willingness to step in for a moment, but that he must be going soon-as ha had several things to do. After rummaging in a cupboard the old sailor, with a wink, produced a small bottle of cham- pagne. He extracted the cork with deliberation, and beamed with satisfaction as the golden liquid ran creaming into the glasses. "Well, here's luck to the lass and my own boy," he said, "and another ten thousand a-year to you, Mr. Hale." The latter responded, and felt in spite of him- self the better for the cheery greeting and a gulp of the generous liquid which he had just imbibed. Captain Latham filled the tumblers once more, and then launched into a glowing eutogium of the wine. His praises, however, were cut short when he noticed an expression of con- sternation and astonishment on the face of his guest, whose eyes were following some object on the floor, a little to the rear of the captain's right foot. "What's the matter ? " he said. "What is that, captain?" asked Abraham Hale, in a shaking voice. Captain Latham turned. A small black monkey was sitting on the floor behind him, un- concernedly busied about some detail of his. toilet. "Hullo, Jacko!" said the captain. The animal turned its head at the sound of the captain's voice, chattered in an amiable fashion with its teeth, and then bounded, with the agility of one of those acrobats which nature trains in the forest, on to his master's shoulder; where he sat, to all appearance, very contentedly. "My son brought him home last voyage," said Captain Latham. "He's a good little beast and rare fond of me; ain't you, jacko ?" He scratched the monkey dexterously with the forefinger of his right hand. i "I didn't know you had a monkey," said Mr. Halo, in a tone which he strove to render in. different.

"No, We didn't let him out much till he got used to the place, for fear of losing him. He did get out of the house on, evening—the evening I came to see you about—" Captain Latham suddenly remembered what an unpleasant experience that interview had been, and turned the conversation in embarrass- 11lent.

"Ili! Jacko," he cried, "salute the gentle- man."

The monkey raised his hand to his head, on which was perched a species of forage cap trimmed with imitation gold lace. He lifted the cap gravely from his head and made a courteous bow to Mr. Hale. The conversation was continued after this, but Mr. Hale was obviously not in the mood for talking, and it was not long before he rose to tal:o his departure. "Well, I hope we shall see something of each other in the future," said Captain Latham, amiably.: "we're so alone and all that, don't youl now, that it really would be a pity ii we didn't." The hatchet was buried, no doubt, but why as he made his way to the Anchor for a quiet pipe and a glass of his favourite tipple did Mr. Hale mutter to himself more than once: "Abraham, you've been and made a blessed fool of yourself ? THE FASHIONABLE VEIL

Many daring colours and designs are shewn in the newer and more expensive patterns in 'veils. If the hat is white, the veil is black and white, of close mash and dotted with black chenille. If the hat is black, the veil is of the same colour, and a brown veil is worn with a brown hat. Veils were never more in vogue, and they were never more varied or more beautiful, declares the Tribune. The face veils come in hundreds of designs, and just what kind of veils are most becoming each individual has the opportunity of deei(iing for herself. The heavily-dotted, thick veils are extremely smart, and are charming with velvet and furs, while one of the smartest or the new Paris veils has but three or four dots to the yard, each ono fully as large as a shilling. The double-threaded mesh veil, with its chenille dots, is most becoming. It makes the complexion look clear, brings out all the soft shades of the hair, and makes the waves look smoother. With the new toques veils of lace are sometimes worn, but this veil, with an embroidered edge, must never be worn close against the face. The ends should be drawn back carefully, where they are tied, doubled under the hat, and /securely fastened. The spider-web veil is being worn with the large picture-hats. This veil is extremely thin, and serves more than anything to preserve the smoothness of the hair dressing. A SMART COSTUME.

Our sketch illustrates a very smart and up-ro• dato costume of fine faced cloth in a charming shade of peacock-green. The coat, which is almost severely plain, is cut in the very latest style, and fastens in front with a single row of immense and very handsome buttons. From the neck turns back a simple collar of black

MOIRE ANTIQUE BACK AGAIN.

The revival of moire antique provides us women with a most effective and beautiful material, remarks a Globe writer. Why we ever gave it up it is really difficult to imagine. Its lustrous sheen brings out to perfection the lovely tones of Colour in which the moire is made. Take, for instance, a pearl grey, shot with rose and forget-me-not blue. This is the material of which a young wife's dinner gown for her first dinner party as hostess is made. The skirt is arranged in a long flat pleat in front, the rest being skilfully made so that there is no fulness about the hips. The train is not too long. The bodice, cut square, as so many dinner gowns are now, is bolero under the arms, over cream• coloured embroidered net, the design run with gold thread. The bolero is edged with small gold and palest mauve round silk "drops," and round the shoulders is a tucker of tulle to match the mauve and gold. Moire looks better in pale colours than in more pronounced. In the for- mer it looks new and up-to-date, in the latter old-fashioned.

TO-DAY'S REortz.

HARE SOIIP.—At this time of the year old hares are frequently on the market. These are excellent for making soup, or they are delicious when treated se follows: After aleaning and skinning the hare, put it on a baking dish. Sprinkle it with salt and pepper, put some pieces of butter over it, and then bake. When cold take all the meat off the bones, and pound it well in a mortar, rub it through a hair sieve acid sass, mace, and pepper to taste. rut a little fresh butter and a few spoonfuls of the gravy loft from cooking the hare into a saucepan, and warm until the butter is melted; moisten the meat with this, pack it into small pots, and oey•r with warmed butter.

Scores and scores of cases of stubborn skin disease have done the same, but they have all surrendered completely, just as Mrs. Perks' case has done, to the invin- cible healing power of Zarn-lluk.

"lam 56..3T'ars of age," said Mrs. Perks when

, interviewed by a London pressman, " and for seven years I suffered terribly from varicose ulcers. I had what seemed to be little more than a slight rash on my foot. It was, of course, irrit- able and J naturally scratched it. The result was that a day or two afterwards I noticed that. the rash was spreading, and a number of small water blisters forming. These were very pain- ful, and my stocking, or something else, appar- ently poisoned my foot, for very soon it started to swell, and then my leg became much enlarged. "Soon afterwards sores formed and discharged matter. This alarmed me and I consulted a doctor, but he did me no good whatever. I suffered terrible agony, and some of the ointments I tried seemed to burn my flesh like hot iron. "This went on for some time, when a friend suggested I should try Zam-Buk. SHUNTING FATALITY SEQUEL.

PARR'S BANK. THE FINANCIAL CRISIS IN AMERICA. Bright Trade Prospects.

The forty-third annual meeting of Parr's Bank, Limited, was held in Cannon-street Hotel, London, on Thursday. There was a small attendance of share- holders.

Mr. Cecil P. Parr, the chairman, moved the adoption of the report and the declaration of a dividend of 19 per cent., together with a bonus of 1 per cent. This time last year, when commenting on trade prospects, he remorked, If I had any misgiving it was lest the very esubentnce of trade should bring about its own restric- tion and it should suffer from the stringency which might arise from any unprecedented demand on the world's supply of gold," and he added that even if this did occur it might prove a blessing in disguise. His prophetic misgiving had come most liter Illy to pass : he could not have described more completely and accurately what really happened than in his words a year ago.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
HOPEFUL VIEW OF TRADE PROSPECTS.

He might be unduly optimistic, but he did not anticipate, as many people did, a depression in trade in , the immediate future. It was overtrading, over-pro- ' duction which caused depression, and at present he saw hut few signs of that. A veer ago demand was out- stripping supply with the inevitable result of a general , rise in prices, including wages and the cost of raw ! materials. That in its turn necessarily brought about a slackening of demand, which was still further accentu- ated by the increasing stringency of the Money market, and so undoubtedly there was a decided set-baek in ' trade at the moment. But it was not a depression ; he would rather say that we were marking time until the , stringency was over-passed and prices had adjusted ' themselves on lower levels. The world's require- ; ments were still unsatisfied, and in spite of the large loss of capital consequent on the late wars and of the very large unproductive capital which was being , expended in preparation for future wars, the world was richer now than it ever was before, and was ready and , anxious to have its wants satisfied. In America, in : Canada. in Mexico, in Argentina there were not only I railway companies, but many industrial concerns, asking already or prepared to ask for more capital I wherewith to extend their mileage,their power, or their plant, as soon as they could obtain that capital on cheaper terms ; and it was reasonable to suppose that , they saw a profit in these extensions. Money was ; already becoming cheaper. The Bank rate had just been lowered to 4 per cent., and the only fear now was lest all these companies might be asking too quickly for , money, and for too much of it.

THE CRISIS IN AMERICA. Even in the United States the trouble was not brought about by over-trading or over-production ; it arose really from the dislocation of a faculty and inelastic currency system. The enormous expansion of trade not only in America. but throughout most of the countries of the world, called for a corres- ponding expension of the circulating medium. Now, although America possesses more gold than any other country in the world—over 300 millions—that additional and requisite supply was not forthcoming. Perhaps even this stringency would not of itself have been sufficient to precipitate a crisis had not the enormous commitments of speculative millionaires stretched the tension to breaking-point. The situation culminating in panic and in the hoarding of gold was in no sense industrial, but purely a financial deadlock, although of necessity trade and indnstry suffered grievously because of it. It was the want of cash and the temporary paralysis which that want pro- duced which forced many industries to close their I doors, although they could show a good surplus of assets over liabilities. These businesses had not necessarily been over-trading in the past, and they were / only waiting for normal conditions before they resumed , production. Doubtless much money had been made ' and lost again by the inflation and subsequent deflation ! of all kinds of securities, and in consequence the demands for luxuries, such as diamonds, motor-cars, and silks, would be for some time to come very materially lessened. But

===== 0002601_19080125_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RESOURCES OF TIIE UNITED STATES

as a country had been almost insignificantly impaired. The industrial situation there was still sound in spite of many disclosures of roguery and corruption in high places. And although there were many black spots,and perhaps many black sheep in America, it was not really the sink of financial and industrial iniquity that was Sometimes depicted in this country. There were just as many'honest men and sound concerns there as here. It had been computed by a high authority that the annual produce of the soil—cotton, corn, and other crops—of America was on an average worth a thousand millions a year. Now, America had had several good years in succession, so when they took such figures as these, even the losses of which they had had heard a great deal lately, really seemed insignificant, and must be insignificant. Again, when they compared the present crisis with the crisis of 1893 they found that at that time over 190 national hanks closed their doors, and over fifty of these never opened them again whereas while this time only sixteen banks altogether suspended, and most of them were prepared to resume business, while some had actually resumed business alraeciy. Of course, it might easily happen that New York might take arin this year more gold from us. America, it must be remembered, exported to us over .£125,000,000 sterling a year, and she took hack only £25,000,000. Therefore, when they had made allowances far repayments in the matter of freights pnd securities it left a large balance, which left America both the right and the power to take gold from us if she liked. MAIM people in this country had been greatly scandalised at the financial misdoings of our American cousins and 'we told them glibly," Now is the time to place your !currency system on a sound and scientific basis." That ; I was very excellent advice, hut it hardly came with a good grace from us whose system was scarcely less rigid than their own, and whose only resource when a similar calamity overtook them was to break the law.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OUTLOOK IN LANCASHIRE.

When the period of cheaper money intervened—and it had Illraady begun—and when prices adjusted them- selves on the lower leveb it was probable that trade conditions would reassert themselves. He did not, of course, look forward to a year such as they had just had.when trade and traffic returns had broken all records. But the reports they got from all the industries with which they were more particularly connected, especially ' . - in the north, indicated that, almost without exception, the prospects were that business would be large and remunerative in results.' Cotton spinners had not secured such profits as they made in 1907 within a a generation. Colliery owners, ironmasters, the chemical trade both in St. Helens and at Widnes, ; the glass industry at St. Helens. boot mak- ing and hosiCry at Leicester—all had good reason to congratulate themselves on the very successful ! results of the year which had just closed. And, with perhaps tbe exception of the iron trade, the immediate I future of which was perhaps a little uncertain, all these ' trades, as well as many minor trades, such as wire- drawing, mill machinery, silk, tanning, were looking forward with a good deal of confidence not to anything in the nature of a boon. but to good trade conditions, which would result in a satisfactory and reasonable profit. Turning to the balacce sheet, Mr. Parr said he did not think the directors had ever presented a stronger Owing to the unsettled financial conditions prevailing in America and elsewhere, they had felt it prudent to keep themselves more than usually strong in short money, and in December cash and short money amounted to £11,850,000, an increase of nearly .C 1,750,000. If they added their investments, which amounted to £3.410,000, they had a total of avail- able assets well over Si per cent, of their liabilities to the public. The deposits were £BOO,OOO more than a year ago. The increase both in the number of share- holders and of customers was somewhat above the average. The libel action which the hank was bringing against a Society paper had, in keeping with the pro- verbial delays of the law, been pot off several times, but it would now come on for hearing in less than a week. Mr. A. J. Fraser, the deputy chairman, seconded the motion, and the report was adopted unanimously with- oat discussion. On the motion of 'iTr. B. W. Nix, seconded by Sir J. Christopher Gamble, the retiring directors were re-clected.

personal advantage that a candidate can possess. He was able, earnest, uncommonly well-informed, charming in manner, and more than usually indus- trious in visiting the various parts of the constit- uency. No candidate could have worked harder ; none could have been more popular with those electors whcm he was able to influence. Probably if he had to contest the fight over again he would be victorious by force of his vivacity, ability, and attractiveness; to say which is to admit that in our opinion be was beaten because, until the vacancy was created, he was unknown to the electors.

But undoubtedly the defeat was brought about by a combination of all the causes alleged in explanation of it. Some of these were sufficient in themselves. For instance, if the out-voters had not been almost all on one side, or if theylhad not poured into the constituency en masse, the seat would now have been in possession of the Liberal party. Similarly, if the electors had not been deliberately deceived by the election literature countenanced and paid for by the successful candidate, and admittedly of a character such as ought not to be employed in elections, the result might have been different from what it was. In a rural constituency a considerable effect is produced by the armies of agents and organisers poured into it at election times by the Tariff Reform League and other reactionary bodies These persons are paid, and those who pay them doubtless get their money's worth in misrepresenta- tion ; but they are not paid by the candidate who profits by their services, and who thus gets the benefit of such help as would not be available under a strict interpretation of the Corrupt Practices Act. The rowdyism which accompanied the election can have done no good to Mr. BUXTON ; but for this rowdyism he had no responsibility. It was an accident of which there is no sufficient explanation, except that a large. proportion of the electors in the principal town of the constituency seem to have been "spoiling for a fight." When the suffragettes claim the glory of the victory over the Liberal candidate we can only answer with a smile. The new movement has hardly yet advanced so far as that.

The one indisputable lesson of the contest is that which we named first of all, and which is insisted upon with some grimness of humour by Mr. ARNOLD LUPTON, M.P., who had the distinction of beating Mr.HENRY CHAPLIN in a county constitu- ency. The member for the Sleaford Division certainly ought to be acquainted with the condition of success in such cases, and he explains every- thing by saying "Mr. BUXTON was only before the constituency a few weeks ; the Conservative candidat, had been before the constituency for several years." We are not among those who maintain that a candidate should always, or even in most instances, be a local man. Tlll.t seems to us to be an unintelligent dogma. Nothing Could be more stupid than the employment of the cry of " carpet-bagger "as an argument. Most of our leading men on both sides might be so described —by a goose. However, where the circumstances are not exceptional, the fact that a midi date is not known in the constituency until a vacancy occurs must always tell heavily against him. "The electoral pendulum," as Mr. ARNOLD LEPTON cynically observes, "swings the way it is pulled hardest "; and we agree with the same authority that a candidate for a large rural con- stituency should make the personal acquaintance of every elector, which cannot be effectively done much under a couple of years. This consideration is one that should be steadily kept in mind by Liberal organisers everywhere. A good candidate, in the sense in which Mr. RODEN BUXTON was a good candidate, is often far from being the best candidate for the occasion.

The new Warrington THE DEThignvo Parliamentary Dabs:jug SOCIETY. Society has 3t last been launched on the political sea. As is fit and proper, the Liberal Party takes office for the first session and a Government has been formed which possesses interesting possi- bilities and Considerable debating power. Mr. John Upson ought to make a capital Premier , possessing as he does so many of those solid' qualities which characterise the Premier of England, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman. The Opposition is likely to be in strong force and the Socialist-Labour wing is eager to apply the spur. The Session will open on Thursday evening next when Mr. H. W. Guest will introduce the King's Speech. There after weekly meetings will follow, and, we hope, attract a strong membership of ardent politicians and social reformers. The new Society bids fair to make an auspicious start, and with ordinary luck should acquire the prestige of its predecessor of 25 years ago.

Every by-election in War- ST. AUSTIN'S WARD rington is being turned BY-ELECTION. into a miniature General Election by the Couserva- Mrs party. They are so determined to hold the reins of power locally that they deliberately prevent the electors from choosing the man who would best serve their special interests as a member of the Town Council, by foisting upon the Ward party nominees of the deepest blue dye. The circumstances of the recent by-election in Su. Austin's Ward were somewhat remarkable. Mr. Broadhurst, a humble workingman candidate, was the Conservative nominee, and he was supported night after night by influential Tories like Sir Gilbert Greenell, Mr. R. Pierpoint, and Mr. W. P. Rylands, who prated loudly about imperial and colonial affairs, religious education, and other matters which do not strictly concern a ward repre- sentative. They were as keen on securing Mr. Broadhurst's return as they would have been if he had been contesting the representation of the Borough in Parliament. It makes one wonder why, and at the same time suggests no con- clusion that the Tory party in Warrington is seeking to fill the Council Chamber with men who will toe the line at the dictates of wire-pullers outside. This sort of thing is not conducive to good local government. It does not need half an eye to see that the rory party is being financed and bolstered up by the brewers, and that it is they who are really getting the administration of the town into their hands. Mr. Magian has long been a determined opponent of the privileged brewing interest, hence the special efforts put foith to beat him on Monday. He is to be congratulated on making a plucky fight for principle and independence in municipal life, but until the electors use their eyes to look beneath the glittering cloak of Tory.tactics and see the sinister form beneath there will be little hope of an enlightened and public-spirited Town Council.

That the year has taken NAT UR z the turn towards spring the IN WINTER. increased light at half-past seven in any morning, or half-past four in any afternoon, henceforth cheer- fully proclaims. There is the greater invitation to take one's walks abroad, over common or through woodland. The peaceful beauty of spinney or copse is, as expressing that mood, more attractive now than at any other time of the year. The wood is as lovely in winter as in high summer, and beside the profound calm of its stately aisles there is to-day the stirring of new life in the later sun- shine, the: bird's pipe, and the rustle of under-

growth, which already, in imagination, can place at the foot of some smooth bole the star of the primrose. He is an urban woodman who can only individualise the trees when they are in foliage. The top-heaviness of the elm, the self. sufficient power of the "horizontal " oak, the rounded trunk and springing hues of the beech, the crooked boughs of walnut and plane, these are as readily to be known in winter as at other times. The traceried branches make their exquisite address against a grey sky no less than when the leaves are bursting their sheaths in sunshine and azure. The collective word of the countryside is that winter brings neither gloom nor despondency. By woodland lake or under the bowling wind upon the heath Nature is always the Socratic philo- sopher. She is ever good at propounding questions, and equally from untroubled calm and the turbulency of " wild west wind " issues the com- fortable reminder, " If winter come can spring be far behind ?"

RATS.

The latest crusade is one to exterminate the plague of

rats, which is estimaed to do damage in this country to the extent of £10,000,000 annually. At one time the common rat of Great Britain was the black Mus rattus. But it has disappeared before the brown Mus decumanus from Norway, a more enterprising and stronger species. So prolific is this rodent that it has spreal over the greater part of Europe, and is equally common in America. The way in which rats infest ships and breed in them in spite of all endeavours to get rid of them has always been a remarkable testimony of their migratory propensities. At the same time, their instinct which causes them to leave a ship suddenly has always been an indication that the vessel is destined to meet with a mishap during her forthcoming voyage. At one time witches were believed, after Ithey had been burnt, to come to life again in the form of rats, but such animals never had tails. Thus, in Denmark, where rewards are paid for the killing of rats by the number of tails handed in, the witches hitherto have escaped. In the same way, Manx cats which have no tails were formerly looked upon as rein- carnations of wicked women, and never petted. One of the richest provinces of Holland was once inundated and ruined by a hole made in the dykes by a single rat. But we are told that at the present time there are millions of rats infesting the London docks. If, therefore, one rat has been capable of devastating an entire Dutch province, if all the rats in the city of London rose in revolt against the present scheme of exterininating them imagination boggles over the mischief they may be capable of doing.

THE EXAMINER.

JANUARY 250, 1908.

It is hardly worth while SOCIALISM (says the "Westminster AND Gazette ") to follow the LABOUR, various explanations by

which different Labour leaders endeavour to give a show of coherence to the action of their Conference in rejecting a Socialist resolution on Tuesday and accepting a Socialist resolution on Wednesday.. The simple fact is that the Socialists somehow secured their revenge for the vote which went overwhelm- ingly against them on the previous day. The voting at the Conference is, of course, in any event a somewhat artificial proceeding, "The resolution means nothing," Mr. WILL THORNE is reported to have said : " it is merely an expression of opinion on the part of the dele- gates who are in favour of Socialism. It does not affect the constitution of the party. Therefore, no candidate can be compelled to accept i when he stands." Which means that, while the Labour Party avows as "its definite object the Socialise- tion;of the means of production, distribution, and exchange," members,in joining it, or organisations in becoming federated to it, are not bound to agree with that object or to pledge themselves to pursue it. This, we are bound to say, out-Balfours Mr. BALFOUR ; and, if it is to be the " definite " attitude of the Labour Party, it would go far to cover it with the same kind of discredit as has recently been attached to the Conservative Party. Mr. SHACKLETON talked some plain sense on this subject to the Conference on Wednesday, and we hope the more sober of trade unionists who have hitherto refrained from asserting them- selves, will be encouraged by his example to mak3 their position plain. No party can be in a strong ' position with its own members, and still less in a strong position before the public, if its policy is to be the" definite "indefinite as interpreted by Mr. THORNE. The Labour Party, if we can say so with respect, seems at this moment to be peculiarly in need of strong leadership. Socialists of the middle-class who dream dreams, who are well versed in Socialist economics, but who are not in thought or habit of life at one with the real working-class, have to a large extent obtained control of the party and taken the lead from the trade unionists proper. Now, the result of that, if it goes far, will be for years to come to make the movement a kind of academic bogy, academic because it will be given over to quite unfertile declarations of theory and ultimate policy, and a bogy because these declarations will admirably suit the purpose of Conservative or Protectionist politicians seeking to make a re-action.

That is what thinking politicians of the working- class have to consider very carefully at this moment. The Socialists may have their triumphs at the Conferences ; there may be great scenes of cheering and congratulation at the progress which the cause is making, and all kinds of uncom- promising doctrines may be affirmed. But their movement on these lines will be, for all practical purposes, as academic as if it were conducted by dons from University common-rooms. It will, indeed, be worse than academic, for while they are talking about the Socialisation of the means of production and distribution, their party will have lost its strength in Parliament, the House of Lords will have triumphed for another generation, the tariff will have been fastened on the back of the working-class, and all the detailed schemes of social reform which might have been forwarded by a little concentrAted I effort will have lost their chance and have gone by the board. We will put aside the words Socialism and Individualism, and say simply that the move- ment will have passed fronaractical hard work at the daily problem into rheditic and theory. That has been the catastrophe of Continental Socialism, and we may see its results at the present moment in Germany, where the Socialists, having killed the Liberal Government, have for years been unable ,to make any impression on the Conservative ' forces. It is clear, even from the voting on Wednesday's resolution, that a large number of English Labour Members see this danger and wish to avoid it ; but what has happened this week should be a warning to them that they cannot stand idly by and allow events to drift out of their control.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Dense fog seems to be essential to Conservative success in Warrington by-elections. The Tories won both Latchford and St. Austin's Wards during a fog which was so dense that neither the light of heaven nor the light of truth could reach the embarrased voter.

* 0 The Solicitor-General for Scotland has accepted a ehallenge to debate Socialism with Mr. Bruce Glasier, the editor of "The Labour Leader." The discussion will take place at Annandale, and the arrangements will be made by the local branch of the Labour party. No date has yet been fixed for the debate. 1* * * Good health and good luck to Sergeant Evans on his retirement from the Police force after 27 years service ! Sergeant Evans was a courteous, efficient, and popular officer. He distinguished himself by his organisation of the annual police sports and it is gratifying to learn that he will continue to give his services in this direction.

* * The Vicar of Leamington he Rev. W. A. Buck), writing on the visit of the Mayor and Corporation to the parish church in his Parish Magazine, says :—"So long at least as the Church of England is the national and established Church, there can be no doubt at all as to the proper church for such an official service." Yet there are some Church Defence quibblers who deny that their Church is established at all. * * * On his homeward trip by tramcar a tired business man was much annoyed by the conduct of three middle-aged ladies who stood near him. All the seats in the car were occupied, but they seemed determined that he should offer one of them his seat. He screened himself behind his paper and listened to plainly, audible remarks about the decline of gallantry in the present age. This grated on his nerves, so he rose, and with a profound bow, addressed the three : " Will the eldest of you ladies honour me by acceptins my place ? " Whereupon they became interested in the advertisements over the windows, and the man triumphantly resumed his seat. Tre irgalilt4 ** * Bargaining by the brewers is once more in the air,and at the private meeting of magistrates yesterday an offer by Messrs. Greenall and Co. to give up two off- licences in exchange for the transfer of the licence of the Bridge Inn (which will presumably have to come down for improvements) to Gorsey-lane, was con- sidered but, by no means favourably. Needless to say there will be tremendous opposition to the granting of a new licence in Gorsey-lane district, which contains the new Beamont School. Some of the temperance bodies have already passed resolutions of protest, and the magistrates will be bound to proceed very cautiously in the matter. The desire of the brewers to exploit a large and growing 1 1 district is understandable, but their aim is not on all fours with the best interests of the public. * * * It is believed that Mr. Arthur Henderson will be the new leader of the Labour party in the House of Commons.Mr.Shackleton has announced that he would not accept the position and he would be still less likely to take it after the vote of Wednesday. Mr. Ramsay Macdonald prefers the unobtrusive position of secretary to the party, and Mr. Barnes, whose name was also mentioned, is not in good health. So for as a Labour member can be called moderate (says the London Correspondent of the "Liverpool Post and Mercury ") Mr. Henderson deserves the description, but, of course, he will have to voice the claims and demands of his party. He is himself a man of good sense—a Radical, in fact, gone slightly wrong —and I have no doubt he will exert himself to prevent his party from pursuing wild and erratic courses. The worst thing about cycicism is its truth.- 312.:7)zer.

If men should rise from the dead and read their epitaphs many would think they had got into the wrong grave.—Fla eel. * * * " Woe to the wretch who fails to rear

At this dread sign the ready spear !" This is the motto of the Scottish Nationalists, a copy of whose journal "The Fiery Cross" has reached us. It seems odd, and not a little amusing, to read nowadays of the efforts of a Jacobite organisa- tion to supplant our "present de facto monarch " with a representative of the House of Stuart. Yet there are apparently a number of proud Scotchmen who " as true Loyalists . . . advocate the restoration of our ancient legitimate and hereditary Stuart family, now lineally represented by 11.R.H. the Princess of Bavaria, whose legitimate title is Queen Mary 111. of Scotland and Great Britain and IV. of England, France and Ireland." Among minor demands by these perfervid Scots are the " restoration of our national parliament (the Three Estates) of which our nation was defrauded by an illegal Convention in 1700," and the elimination of " St. George and the Dragon "from the British coinage, " as it is a gross breach of international rights and courtesy, and implies the unwarrantable subjection to England of the sister nationalities of Scotland and Ireland." All this makes one fe-3I quite a usurp,‘r and oppressor. We hope no" wietcla " will " raise the ready spear ' ' round here or, as Mark Twain would say, it will go hard with him !

A grand evening concert in aid of the Infirmary will be given by Mrs. W. B. Roberts's Ladies Orchestra and other .rtistes in the Parr Hall, on Saturday February 22nd. In connection with the Pan-Anglican Conference, a public meeting will be held in the Parr Hall on Thursday February (sth. A juvenile ball in aid of the fund for the purpose of foundin a children's cot in the Warrington Infirmary Extension will be held in the Parr Hall on February 13th from 7 to 11 p.m. Crosfield's band will play for dancing. Full particulars advertised. LINSEED COMPOUND 'of 40 years' proven efficacy for Coughs, Colds, and Bronchial troubles.--Advt. Illness of Mr. W. El. Houghton.

We regret to hear that Mr. W. D. Houghton, of the firm of Messrs. W. D. Houghton and Sons, Sankey Bridges, had a paralytic seizure on Monday at his residence in Colwyn Bay. Since then he has pro- gressed as favourably as could be expected. Big Temperance Meeting. A great mass meeting in favour of local option is announced to take place in Parr Hall on Wednesday, February 19th, when the speakers will include Mr. T. H. Sloane, MP. (Belfast), Mr. John Ward, M.P. (Hanley), Captain Richard Rigg, M.A., Canon Hicks, M.A. (Manchester). The chair will be occupied by Mr. James Dodds. Women's Suffrage. A big demonstration in favour of Women's Suffrage was held in the Queen's Hall, London, last night under the auspices of the Women's Liberal Federation. The meeting was addressed entirely by ladies, and the following telegram was sent to the meeting on behalf of the Warrington Association by Mrs. E. Osborne (hon. se3retary) : " Warrington Women's Liberal Association heartily supports Women's Suffrage and wishes demonstration every success." The Presentation to Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield The presentation to the Borough Member and his wife, subscribed to by all sections of the Liberal party in Warrington, will be made at a great Liberal demonstration in Parr Hall, on Wednesday February sth. Mr. C. E. Parker will preside and Mrs. Parker will make the presentation. A grand concert will follow, the artistes being Miss Alice Woodland (soprano), Miss Lily Fairhurst (contralto), Mr. Harris (bass), Mr. J. Rabbit (tenor), the Lyric Prize Quartet, and the Co-operative Glee Party. Mr. J. Forster will be the accompanist. " Under the Red Robe." Commencing on Monday, February 10th, and during the week, the Warrington Dramatic Society will present Mr. Stanley Weyman's romantic play "Under the Red Robe." Some fascinating performances should result, and the public is promised special scenery and appointments. The proceeds will be devoted to the Infirmary Extension Fund and the Children's Summer Camp Fund. It is worthy of note that the Society has, during its distinguished career, raised over £2,000 for local charities. The plan for seats for the coming performances opens at Mr. H. M. Ashton's 63, Sankey-street, on February Ist, at 11 a.m.

LINSEED COMPOUND,' Trade Maik of Kay Compound Essence of Linseed for Coughs & Colds.--Aav Neglect of Children. John and Bridget M`Coy, 3. Hopwood-street, were charged at the Warrington Borough Court, on Thurs- day, with neglecting their two children.-11r. Arthur Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., and the evidence showed that Inspector Cox had repeatedly warned the prisoners, who were of drunken habits, about neglecting their children. The neglect had reached such a pitch, however, that it was impossible to allow it to go any further, because the children were absolutely starved. On Tuesday Inspector Cox ; visited the house and found the two children huddled , I together on a filthy straw mattress. They were dirty and ravenously hungry, and an order was made for them to go to the Workhonse. The prisoners could 1 not be found on that day. At the Workhouse the children were given some bread and milk, which they I eagerly devoured. The male prisoner was lazy and ' would not work, and there was no doubt that unless • some action had been taken the children would have ,- been starved to death. The woman was worse than the man. Each prisoner was sent to gaol for three months with hard labour.

STREAKY BACON 6d. per lb. at AsHLEY's.—Advt. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—IINDER THE CLOCK VOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Promenade Concert.

A successful promenade concert, arranged by Mr. Levi Richardson, was held in the Conservative Club Assembly Room on Wednesday evening. There was a • large attendance and the programme met with cordial appreciation. The chief item was a song cycle "The Sunbonnet " (Lohr) in which the vocalists were Miss M. P. Rigbv,Miss Avico Beswick,Mr.Levi Richardson, and Mr. Harry Davis, with Mr. George S. Webb (St. Michael's, Liverpool) as accompanist. This song cycle is a very bright and pleasing work and its interpretation was thoroughly adequate. Miss Lena Duckworth sang " The merrie month of May" (Newton) very greditably. The part songs "The Lassie of Arranteenie " (IV. 8. Roddie) by Miss Rigby, Miss Beswick, Mr. T. E. Knowles and Mr. Wilfred Rigby, and " Moonlight " (John 11. L. Glover) by Miss Duckworth, Miss Besvrick, Mr. T. E. Knowles and Mr. Rigby proved popular features. Mr. Levi Richardson fully maintained his high reputa- tion in " Lend me your aid " (Gounod), and responded to a hearty encore with " I'll sing to thee of Araby" (Clay.) Miss Rigby sang " Spring is here " Wick) with pleasing effect and Mr. Harry Davis was heard to great -advantage in " The little Irish girl " (Lohr) and " Song of the Day" (Aylward). Mr. J. Jordan created great merriment with some capital humorous songs. Mr. Hugh Mellor was an able accompanist. During the intervals Mr. T. Boothroyd gave some admirable gramophone selections.
