All Satin& Church, Thelwall, was the scene of a pretty wedding on Tuesday afternoon when Dr. James Hay Shepherd, of Aysgarth, second son of the late Mr. George Shepherd, of Shethin, Tarves, Aberdeen- shire was married to Miss Cecilia Dorathea Morris, only daughter of the late Rev. E, S. Morris, of York, and of Mrs. Morris of Sling,aby, York. The chancel of the church was beautifully decorated with flowers. The service, which was choral, Was conducted by the Rev. A. S. Brooke, rector of Slingsby, York, assisted by the Rev. T. Pyrn Williamson, vicar of ThelwalL The bride was given away by her cousin Mr. W. Peter Rylands. She was charmingly attired in soft ivory satin, trimmed with Honiton and Carrickmacross lace, and her bouquet was composed of lilies of the valley, tulips, and lilac. Ths bridesmaid was Miss Amy Shepherd, sister of the bridegroom, who wore a dress of biscuit-coloured eolienne, trimmed with green velvet, with a green hat to match, and carried a bouquet of pink carnations. Dr. H. Leith Murray, of Liverpool acted as best man. Mr. W. B. Cook was at the organ. After the ceremony Miss Morris and Mrs. Rylands, aunts to the bride, were the co-hostesses at a reception held at Massey Hall. Later Dr. and Mrs. Shepherd departed for London to spend the honeymoon, the bride travelling in a green tailor made costume with hat to match.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT. -- Formation of Liberal Ministry. The newly formed Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society will get to business on Thursday evening next, when the first debate will be held in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street. The local Liberal party has made full preparations :for the wordy war- fare, and a strong Government will take up the reins of office. A large and enthusiastic meeting of Liberals was held at Atkinson's Cafe, Bridge-street, on Tuesday evening, for the purpose of electing a Premier and transacting other preliminary business. The Rev. W. Mellor, of Padgate, presided. On the motion of Mr. J. F. AWBERY, seconded by Mr. W. FCRNESS, it was resolved that the Liberal party take office. .

The next step was the election of Premier and the unanimous choice of the meeting fell upon Mr. John Upson who was elected on the proposition of Mr. W. Furness, seconded by Mr. E. England and supported by Mr. G. Walker. _

There is no doubt that Mr. Upson will fill his exalted office in a capable manner, for he is a ready speaker and all who have heard him on the Education Committee will acknowledge hialebating powers. The following Cabinet appointments have been made : Lord Chancellor : Councillor John Wilkinson. Foreign Minister : Mr. E. Wynne Jones. Home Secretary : Mr. G. Walker. Secretary for War : Mr. P. Peacock. Secretary for Ireland : Mr. J. F. Awbery. Chancellor of the Exchequer : Mr. E. England. President of the Local Government !bard : Mr. J Morton.

President of the Board of Trade : Mr. J. Jarman. First Lord of the Admiralty : Mr. J. Elwell. Colonial Secretary: Mr. W. Furness. Secretary for Scotland : Sherburn.

Postmaster General : Mr. W. Clegg. Whips : Mr. N. Cook, jun. and Mr. J. Howes, jun. We understand that at the first meeting of "Parliament "the King's Speeeh will be moved by Mr. Herbert W. Guest an I Mr. A. Freeman. It will include measures dealing with licensing reform, old- age pensions, education, land values, plural voting' nationalisation of railways and, probably, the Irish question.

EkRLESTOWN GROCERS ASSOCIATION. Last Thursday night the annual dinner and soiree of the above association took place in the Town Hall, Earlestown. The large hall had been very tastefully docorated for the occasion by the staff of Mr. E. T. Evans with flags, banners, and Japanese tapestries. 1 Mr. Sud worth's band discoursed sweet music after dinner and played for dancing later on very satisfactorily. The repast was served by Mr. G. S. King, Bridge-street, to which about 80 shopkeepers, with their wives, friends and assistants sat down. Amongst the visitors were Mr. Sutcliffe (Manchester), Ar. McDonnell, Mr. Jones (Thomas &Co., Liverpool),Mr. Urmson (Cros- field's Warrington), &c. After dinner a short concert took place Misses Barr and Grissold sang "In the tusk of the twilight" very nicely, and the former also joined Mr. J. Molyneux in a capital rendering of " Excelsior." Mr. Molyneux sang "My old shako " and "My queen beloved "very effectively. Miss Barr contributed " Needles and pins," and Miss Grissold "Song of the Muleteer," both items being well received. The humourist was Mr. Teddy Whittle, who sang several items of a humourous natnre, and was generally encored. At nine o'clock dancing commenced and was kept up with vigour for several hours. The committee who made excellent arrangements were Messrs. E. Parkinson, T. Gregory, C. Holt, R. Molyneux, and E. A. ince (secretary), but the last-named was unfortun- ately absent owing to illness.

! RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

A RUNCORN LITIGATIO

On Thursday. before Mr. Justice Channell. in the Ring's Bench Division, Mr. Walter Ryde applied that the non-jury case of the Rnneorn Urban District Council v. The Runcorn Rural District Council might by consent of all parties, stand over and go to the bottom of the non-jury list, as the parties were negotiating for a settlement, and it WAS hoped that the court would be spared the trouble of hearing the ease. The case was adjourned accordingly.

CRUELTY TO A HORSE.

---- - "Charles Mainwaring, a young boatman, was snmmoned at Runcorn Petty Sessions on Vfonday for having cruelly illtreated a horse by thrashing it with a whip on the 16th inst. He pleaded guilty.—X police officer said he saw the defendant loading a horse which was attached to a boat on the canal, between Halton-road and Canal- street. He thrashed the horse with a whip very severely. There was no need for the thrashing becanse the horse was doing it work well. After being struck the horse became frenzied and reared and fell into the canal. He took the defendant to a man on the boat further along the canal, and asked him if he saw the defendant beat the horse. He said he had and told defendant some little time previously to leave the horse alone.—Defendant said the horse was too near the canal and he thrashed it for the purpose of getting it away.—Thomas Hill, horsekeeper at the stables, Top Locks, said the horse was an old one, but a good worker. Defendant had not had it long, and did not know its temper. It nould not have the whip. Witness saw the horse afterwards, and it was all right. It was not marked.—A fine of ss.was imposed.

GIRL'S ALLEGED FORGERY.

; At Widnes on Monday, Mary Platt (18) of ;no fixed abode, but a native of Widnes, was charged with feloniously defrauding Mrs. Mary Roberts by I forging her signature and withdrawing .28 7s. Bd. from ; the bank belonging to Mrs. Roberts.—Superintendent Strickland said Mary Roberts was a married woman residing in Ash-row, Widnes. On August 18 Mrs. Roberts sent defendant to the bank to lodge some money, which made the total up to £6 7s. Bd. The bankbook was returned, and it was locked up in a box ,in complainant's bedroom. Some time in September the defendant and her sister went to Mrs. Roberts' house, and in October Mrs. Roberts went to look for the box and found it had been stolen, as well as £2 ss. in money. Platt was questioned about it, but she said she knew nothing of it. Defendant on October 1 went to the West Bank Post Office and signed a withdrawal notice for 21, and on each of the following dates—the 4th, 9th, 14th, 17th, and 22nd--41e drew £l, and the remaining 7s. Bd. on the 25th. Upon the complainant making inquiries, it was found that all her money in the bank had been withdrawn, and upon the withdrawal notices she identified the handwriting as that of Mary Platt. Platt and her sister absconded the next day, and Mary was apprehended in Blackburn. Upon being charged, she said "Our Edith made me do it. We went to the Post Office. She stayed outside while I got the money." The girl was remanded for a week.

BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. J. Spruce in the chair. There were also present :—Messrs. T. Wight, J. B. Chadwick, S. Woodward, L. Bolton, J. Mvnekyme. C. Rovlance, W. Weir. J. Hallows, E. Britch, J. Stokes, H. Mawdsley, T. Guest, A. Broome, P. Booth, J. W. Timperley, T. Antwis, G. Pilling, A. R. Norman, J. L. Jones, J. Whitlow, E. Bretherton, and G. F. Ashton (clerk),T. Cook,W. R. De Prez and J. Pearson (releiving officers).

RECREATION FOR THE BOYS. Letters were read from certain estate a gents with regard to the application of the Board for a field to be used as a boys' recreation ground, and stating that the agents would give the matter consideration, and would do their best to obtain one.

_ ILLNESS OF THE MASTER. The Clerk said the members were no doubt aware that the Master of the Workhouse was unfortunately ill and he (the Clerk) thought some responsible person should fill the vacancy temporarily. He thought the boys' caretaker ( \lr. Boyce) was capable of the position. Mr, Woodward said he was very sorry to hear of the I condition of the master and he thought it would quite relieve the Cunnah family if some one was appointed. From inquiries that had been made it was found that the caretaker was quite capable of filling the position temporarily. Mr. Spruce, chairman of the House Committee, supported all that Mr. Woodward had said and moved that Mr. Boyce be brought before the board and questioned. (Hear, hear.) The caretaken was then sent for but was not available. He was appointed temporarily. TIIE TREAT POSTPONED. A letter had been received by the Visiting Committee from Miss Dutton stating that ttle Ladies Visiting , Committee desired that the treat which was to have taken place should be postponed till the master had recovered from his illness. ' The chairman thought it would be better to adjourn , the treat, and it was agreed to. RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

ST HELENS.

A COMMON NIJISANCE.

Joseph 111`Naughton, aged twenty-three, who had a record of twenty-one convictions, was charged at St. Helens on Saturday, when it was stated that the previous night he was drunk and disorderly, and abused his father and mother. The Chief Constable said this youth was a rascal. He had been sentenced to six months imprisonment for unlawful wounding, but Ps soon as he got anything to drink he went to the house and kicked up a row. Ile was sentenced to twenty-eight days' imprisonment for drunkenness and ordered to find sureties for twelve months or serve another six months in default. Prisoner: That is rather too much ; it means altogether seven months imprisonment. The Chairman : You are a COlllO3Oll nuisance and disturbance to the whole place.

A ST. HELENS CAB SMASH. FUTILE CLAIM AGAINST THE TRAM COMPANY. 77. g

At St. Helens County Court on Wednesday, before his Honour Judge Shand, a claim for .£1.7 4s. 6d. damages was brought against the St. Helens Tramway Company by Hiss Mary Emma Wardlow, of Bury. The evidence was that on December 22nd, 190 G, plaintiff went from Bury to St. Helens with her sister and brother-in-law and two children. They took a cab to go to Boundary-road, and when they reached Church- street, opposite the entrance to the Parish Church, the cab was overturned by being run into by a tramcar. Heats, the driver, was thrown off and his thigh broken, and the occupants of the cab were hurt. Miss Wardlow had her clothing damaged, and when she returned to Bury she was attended by Dr. Vine. She was a dress- maker, but could not work for over three months. Thomas Kavanagh, Church-street, the driver of the tramcar, said his car at the time was not going more than two miles an hour. The accident was caused by the cabman suddenly jumping his cab across the street in front of the tram. Eight witnesses wore called to speak to the fact that the car was going very slowly, and that it pulled up quickly. Thomas Swift, a carter, said that the cab cut across in front of the tram, and he had to pull his horse on to the footpath out of the way of the cab.—The Judge decided that there was no negligence on the part of the company. He thought probably the accident was due to the rashness of tho cab driver. He gave judgment for defendants, with oats.

THE LATE MISS WILLIS.

IMPRESSIVE SCENES AT THE FUNERAL.

There were impressive and touching scenes at the funeral of the late Miss Cicely d' Anyers Willis, only daughter of the Rev. Canon Willis, Rector of Warring- ton, and Mrs. Willis, which took place at the Cemetery on Monday afternoon. Within the venerable Parish Church a very large congregation gathered for the first part of the burial service which commenced at one o'clock. The assembly was made up of " all sorts and conditions "of people. Representatives of leading families who had come in their carriages, workingmen who had sacrificed part of their dinner hour to be present, school children, clergy—all united to pay a last tribute to the memory of one who was held in high esteem by everyone who knew her. The channel was beautiful with a large number of floral tributes which had been arranged in front of the choir stalls. The full choir of the church was in attendance and their devotional interpretation of the musical parts of the service added much to its dignity and emphasised that hopefulness which was the dominant note. When the coffin was borne into the church the choristers and clergy preceded it along the centre aisle, while the opening sentences were chanted to Troyt's setting. The service was conducted by the Rev. T. A. J. Ridpath, the Rev. A. A. Markham (the recently- , appointed rector of Grimsby, and a former curate of the Parish Church), who recited the prayers, and the Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson, who read the lesson Psalm xxxix was beautifully sung to Reinagle's chant and the hymns were "Brief life is here our portion, "Jerusalem, my happy home" and " Praise my soul the King of Heaven,' the last-named to Goss's inspir- ing tune. Mr. F. M. Darby, Mus. Bac., was at the organ, and at the close or the service played Chopin's majestic Funeral March, followed by " 0 Rest in the Lord." At the head of the procession to the Cemetery walked a number of boy: and girls from the National and Fairfield schools, under the direction of Mr. G. Brciwn and Mrs. Stansfield. Immediately before the hearse came the choir and clergy. On the way from the Cemetery gates to the grave, the hymn "On the Resurrection morning "was sung. The last rites were conductad by the Rev. G. B. Thurston,Rector of Lyrum, and at the close the choir sang " Now the labourer's task is o'er" and the Nunc Dimittis to a setting by Stainer. The grave had been prettily lined with flowers and evergreens sent by the Hon. Mrs. Willis, Mr. and Mrs. Buckton, Miss Gray, and Miss Fairclough (Chester). The chief mourners were the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) and two sons—the Rev. F. E. d'A. Willis, rector of Uffington, Stamford, and Mr. Arthur Willis, of Calcutta • (Mr. Evelyn Willis, another son home from India, was unable to be present, as he was confined to the Rectory with influenza) Mr. Willis and the Edon. Mrs. Willis, lialsnead Park ; Miss Willis and Miss Cecilia Willis, Sandfleld, Hals- head, and Miss Alice Gray, Westbere, Kent (aunts) ; the Rev. H. P. Brown, Syston Rectory, and Mrs. Vernon (cousins) •, Mr. H. Beresford Melville, Dr. Langdale, Mr. and Mrs. Border and the maids from the Rectory. The clergy present were :—The Revs. Canon Steven- son, E. C. E. Carleton, G. B. Thurston, A. A. Markham, Hon. C. F. Cross, Jas. Davies, H. V. Pigot, W. Brace- camp, H. B. Firth, a. C. Briggs, W. Pollock-Hill, A. F. Whi te,G. W. Dickenson, T.A. J. Rid path T. W.Barber( who had been taking duty for the Rev. P. si. d'A. Willis at Uffington during the seek-end, and had been travelling since early morning in order to attend the funeral), G. A. Lejeune, H. A. Lester, G. Vale Owen, F. W. E. Chadwick, F. Howard Pitt (representing the Rev. C. C. Elam, Mr. H. Douglas Horsfall, and the congregation of St. Agnes's' Liverpool), A. Inglis, F. E. Evans, E. P. Wethered, and E. E. Peters. The church officers were represented by Messrs. S. Taylor (people's warden), E. Isherwood, R. W. Collin, IF. G. Taylor, R. W. Antwiss, Councillor Rogers, Coun- cillor Locker, and E. NV. Coates. The Training College was represented by, in addition to the Principal and Vice-Principal, Miss Ferrimau, Miss Timewell, Miss Bell (members of the staff), and twelve students.

There were also present :—Mr. Robert Pierpoint, Mr. Lister Kaye, Mr. H. Greenall, Colonel Buckton, Captain Osborne, Major Appleton, Captain Crosfield, Lieut. Owen, Alderman Bennett, Rev. F. Vaughan, Mr. Charles White, Mr. E. J. Hall, Captain Waring. Mr. VV. Scotson, Mr. F. Taylor, Mr. W. Taylor, Mr. Taylor, Mr. E. C. Cooper, Councillor Nield, Mr. Thorpe, Mr. W. H. L. Gorse, Mr. W. H. An immense number of wreaths were sent by sorrow- ing relatives and friends, among them being one from Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P., and Mrs. Crosfield, and others from various classes and church organizations with which the deceased lady was associated. The funeral arrangements were efficiently carried out by Messrs. W. Hodgkinson, Ltd. Pulpit references of a sympathetic nature were made at the Parish Church on Sunday morning by the Rev. T. A. .1. Ridpath, and in the evening by Canon Morley , Stevenson.

GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. New Century Pictures at Parr Hall. In the world of animated photography, Sidney Carter's New Century Pictures hold a foremost place. Their return visit t , Warrington has been cordially welcomed. The performances have been given every evening this week, and will concinue another week with matinees to-day and Saturday next. The pro- gramme is one of the best of its kind ever presented in Warrington, some of the most wonderful and up-to-date productions in animated photography being shown. Perhaps the most beautiful picture in the programme is " A visit to Niagia Falls," The most pathetic picture is " The Cashier's Loyalty," which shows how the cashier's home was sold up and his attempted suicide after being accused of robbing his employer, but is afterwards entirely cleared from blame. The customs of the people of West Africa are Most realisticalli shown in a picture entitled " Life and scenes in 11 eat Africa." A most dramatic pro- duction is " A brother's sacrifice " whilst the most humorous one is "The recruit." Amongst other items are " A. night in dreamland," "Mystery of a ,cab," " Accidents will happen," or "The new Com- pensation Act," "Two youthful Hackenschmidts," "Adventures of a street singer," " The heavenly twins," " House breaking for love," and " The milliner's dream." The entertainment is organized and directed by Mr. Francis D. Suaderland, and the acting-manager is Mr. John May who also sings several solos with great effect. Those who wish to spend a pleasant and profitable evening should not fail to visit the Parr Hall.

LICENSING MATTERS. IMPORTANT MEETING OF JUSTICES.

The Bridge Inn. An important meeting of magistrates was held at the Police Station yesterday afternoon under the presidency of Mr. John Fairclough. The business included the appointment of the Licensing Committee. Last year the whole of the magistrates took upon themselves the functions of the Committee but yester- day it was decided to revert to the old custom of having a special committee. The following were, appointed to constitute the Licensing Committee : The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) Alderman Tinnion, councillor Wilkinson, Dr. Fox, Messrs. John Fair- Clough, Henry Roberts, J. W. Hayward, C. Jackson Holmes, John Maginn, S. Wright and W. Richardson. Mr. J. J. Firth, -Police Court Missionary, 19, Forest street, Latchford, was appointed probationer officer under the Probation of Offenders Act 1907. _ _

The magistrates have recently had under considera- tion the redundancy of off-licences in the Whitecross and Latchford Wards, and yesterday they were asked to meet for the purpose of receiving from the owners or their representatives personally the result of their conference amongst themselves. It was, we hear, suggested by a sub-committee that the nine off-licences in these districts should be reduced by two. There was considerable discussion, and we understand the outcome was that the Chief Constable has to notify numerous off-licence holders that their licences will be objected to at the Licensing Sessions on February 7th. It is stated that there was an offer from Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., to surrender two off- licences if the Bench would allow the licence of the Bridge Inn to be transferred to Gorsey Lane. We year, however,that the magistrates were not favourably impressed with the proposal. The matter will come up for decision at the Licensing Sessions.

u PALACE.AND HIPPODROME.

Mirth and Melody.

Mirth and melody are foremost in the admirable programme which the enterprising management of this theatre present to their patrons this week. The !entertainment is as lively and interesting as anyone could desire, and is full of that variety which is said to be "the spice of life." It would be impossible not to be amused by that pantomimical absurdity " Neighours," which is performed by Albert and Edmund's Comedy Troupe. Uproarious fun reigns supreme, for the artistes enter into their parts with zest, and the sketch goes right merrily from beginning to end, All the ludicrous situations arise from the fact that some highly genteel people have removed to a cosy dwelling in what they think is an eminently respectable quarter, and they are horrified to find that their next door neighbours are of the most disreputable class. They soon come into collision, and life to the superior folk is for a time almost unbearable. The musical part of the programme is exceptionally pleasing. M.tvis Mavis delights all with her choice selections on the" glassophone ' or musical glasses, and proves herself to be an expert. She also plays " Sings me to sleep " on the glasses with her right hand, accompanying herself on the piano with the left, and another of her accomplishments is to play a selection in perfect harmony on the piano with the right hand alone whilst fanning herself with the left. Wilman also gives a capital instrumental turn. He plays the well-known " Post Horn Gallop " on the post horn in spirited style and further demonstrates his skill on several other instruments. The Satanello trio, sensational acrobatic dancers, are very popular in their clever and original turn and Amos Howard is a splendid coon singer and sand dancer. Wake and Dean, the argumentative pair, create merriment with their capital songs and patter. The animated pictures are, as usual,quite up-to date, a very interesting series depicting the " Abduction by Pirates" while "The veiled beauty" is most laughable. Wild )p and his orchestra do justice to some capital selections.

WYGLIFFE CHURCH.

Annual Congregational Gathering. Success attended the annual congregational gathering in connection with Wycliffe Congregational Church, which was.held in the Memorial Buildings, Edgeworth- street, on Thursday evening. Admirable arrangements had been made by a committee with Mr. Edward Wallington at the head and a thoroughly enjoyable evening was the result. The room was very tastefully decorated with plants, fans and curtains, and presented a bright and inviting appearance. About 220 sat down to tea, the catering of Atkinson's Cafe's, Ltd., giving every satisfaction. The following ladies presided at the tables :—Mrs. Ford, Mrs. Slack, Mrs. Thomas, Mrs. Brooks, Mrs. Williams, Mrs. Haigh, Mrs. Witts, Mrs. Worneraley, Mrs. Berry, Mrs. W. H. Wallington, Miss Brown and Miss Williams. Tea was followed by an entertainment, the Rev. John,Yonge presiding. A most interesting programme was submitted, every item giving much pleasure. The ladies of the choir sang the part songs" Venetian Festa "and " The Minnesingers" with good effect and were warmly applauded. Mrs. G. W. Brooks was heard to distinct advantage in " Roses " and Miss M. Atkinson gave a creditable interpretation of " The fifes of June.' Miss Griffiths sweetly sang " Spirit of Spring" and Miss A. Wallington's expressive 'vocalisation of "Sleep baby sleep "proved a pleasing feature. Miss J. Armstrong delighted the audience with " The Raft" in which her rich voice had good scope. Mr. W. J. Barnes scored a success with " Down the Vale " and Mr. W. H. Wallington was encored for the delightful way in which he sang " The old black mare." Another very acceptable song was " Thora " by Mr. F. Brown and Mr. A. Griffiths gave the ever- popular " Mary of Argyle " in his best style. Mr. B. 'fibbed possesses a powerful bass voice which he used with good effect in " Rock'd in the cradle of the deep." Master R. Atkinson gained cordial rounds of applause with his musical recitation " The 1P69 express " and the readings of Miss Beswick, Miss Corney and Mr. H. H. Ashton were highly enter- taining. A pianoforte duet was well executed by the Misses Slack and Bateman. Mrs. Ilavionr displayed taste and skill in her violin solo "Reverie' and a violin duet by Messrs. T. F. and H. Wallington was exceed- ingly creditable. Mr. T. Ford had the honour of a recall for an excellent concertina solo " Air with variations." .Ir. W. H. Payton, Mrs. Bac., ably dis- charged the duties of accompanist. Durine an interval fruit was served. In a brief address the Pastor called attention to the sale of work which is to be held in April or May and announced that there would be a series of competitions which would be of special interest to the young people. Those desiring particulars were asked to communicate with Miss Walker, High Bank, Fairfield-toad, Latchford. The artistes and workers were cordially thanked on the proposition of Mr. Cavil seconded by Mr. a J. Brown.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR. Edited by John Workman. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Oifice, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week. Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column.

THE TRADES COUNCIL AND ITS CRITICS

There appear to be some differences of opinion as to the aims, ideals and possibilities of the Trades Council and I would suggest that the Secretary of the Trades Council, Councillor Stott, and the Secretary of the L R.C. should state their case, and that "Onlooker," "Spectator "and "Trades Unionist "—all of whom by the way are genuine Trades Unionists, and are not, as was suggested by one writer, "three in one " —should have an opportunity of replying. I have always contended that a properly-conducted Trades Council is a very useful institution, and I should like to see a strong body in Warrinszton with broad ideals. lam afraid that too much time is often wasted in endeavouring to find out points of difference instead of attempting a common platform on which honest, intelligent fellow workers may stand side by side speaking and voting for the good of all. Incidentally, I would like all correspondents to note that a non-de-pludne is adopted in this column for purposes other than abuse, and that those who do sign their names must treat those who do not with the same courtesy and respect as they themselves expect.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
emE

JOHN WORKJIAN.

POVERTY AND 8008.

There are many authors of to-day whose writings are interesting to a reasonably quick mini, and there are some whose charm and power command attention and give delight. But there are not many who com- bine both literary skill and clear gripping fact in such a manner as to gain the attention of the ordinary working man by implanting practical suggestions in new forms and, vesture. Eminent among such writers is H. G. Wells, whose small .11. book on the " Misery of Boots " should be read and read again by every social and industrial reformer. His knowledge of the subject is chiefly due to the fact that the greater part of his childhood was spent in an underground kitchen the window opening on a bricked-in space covered by a grating. So, looking upwards by compulsion of his circumstances, he became acquainted not with the heads and bodies of people, but with their boot soles. The varied types of men and women were represented to him by their style of footwear, and the condition of , same. As he quaintly says : - " Only subsequently, 1 and with, care have I fitted heads, bodies, and legs to ' these pediments." He draws a vivid, sadly humorous picture of the troubles pertaining to cheap new boots, I both while they are new and then the further troubles, inconveniences, chafes, and general cussedness as the inevitable early deterioration sets it. He understands' why the poor town-dweller delighteth not in long sturdy country walks. Long tramps ! The boots would hurt. The bo its would not stand it. Cheap goods produced for profit instead of the real article produced for use and comfort !

" These miseries of boots are no more than a sample. The clothes people wear are no better than their boots ; and the house i they live in far worse. If one expanded the miseries of these things . . . . think of the results of poor, bad, unwise food."

If you my friend have ever tasted the dear delight (not solely feminine) of casting off old clothes, old boots, and replacing same with well made, well fitting garments of the real article, Cm n you not sympathise with his outburst against the idea that these miseries are part of some primordial curse from which there is no escape. The disproof of such an idea is patent. There are people, no more .deserving than others, who suffer from none of these ; things. You may feel you merit no better than to live so poorly and badly, but do Vie little children, the girls, the mass of decent bard-up people deserve no better fate ?

Time was when these despicable insufficiencies of a decent life were compulsory. There was not enough to go round. This cannot be said with truth now, since our produrtive capacity has gromn tremendously. Wherefore I propose to kick some lessons on boots into this column next week.

LAZARUS. MUNICIPAL COAL SUPPLY. A " BURNING " QUESTION.

Sir,—l have read with much interest and above little amusement the Examiner's " Report of the discussion that took place ou Tuesday the 7th instant at the Warrington Town Council on the very important subject of a municipal coal supply. Allow me, Sir, to compliment Councillor Stott on the civic courage and bold initiative manifested by him when moving his resolution on that particular occasion. His pointed,forcible and concise remarks form a welcome contrast to the lengthy, yet timid and comparatively unprofitable speeches that, alas, so often fall from the lips of our " City Fathers." Councillor Stott conclusively proved, tn my mind, that our manufacturers and merchants, many of the municipal and national trading departments of this country, and even the domestic consumers would all receive direct and material benefits by the transference to public control of the colliery industry coupled with the retail sale and distribution of coal.

During the last decade, experience has most decisively proved that Corporations can, as a rule, supply a better gas at a lower price than private enterprise, whilst it is a generally admitted fact that the municipalisation (or public control) of water, baths, trams, electric power, parks, libraries, scavenging, sewage, etc., have all been socially beneficial, either from a commercial, hygienic, or intellectual standpoint.

What reasonable objection can then be urged against a still further extended application of the well-tested principle to thus include the distribution, ownership I and complete control of the coal supply? The Mayor (in opposing Councillor Stott's motion) stated that it was beyond the power of the Council to enter into the gigantic scheme proposed, which would involve them in an expenditure of millions of money ! Of course we all know that it is at present beyond , the power of any local governing body to place under ' public control the Coal Supply. The resolution in question (had it passed) wou3lcl have simply stated that, as the Council believed it would be advantageous to the community were powers granted to municipali- ties to undertake the supply of coal to their inhabitants they, (the Council) invited the co-operation of every Town and City Council in Great Britain, who, through the Association of Municipal Corporations could then jointly " urge the Government to pass legislation giving powers to Municipal Corporations to undertake the sale of coal and the purchase of coal mines." Even supposing (for tbe sake of argument) that this suggested extension of municipal enterprise would need an expenditure of'" millions of money," that does not appear to me a sound objection to the proposed scheme, if it can be demonstrated that such an outlay would be both a profitable and safe investment. ' I would here p )int out to Alderman Smethurst that , during the Boer War, the colliery proprietors of this country formed amongst themselves what is known as a "Ring ;" and mutually agreed to raise the selling price of coal, thus augmenting their private profits by no less a sum than £30,000,000 per annum, and thus indirectly increasing the cost of gas throughout the length and breadth of the land, also very seriously handicapping in the world's markets, our national industry and commerce, and shamefully fleecing the domestic consumers in every part of the United Kingdom. The present enhanced price of coal is largely due to the recent remission of the duty on " exported " fuel, which has given a great impetus to the ,forrign trade, and the coalowners; taking advantage of the exceptional demand, have raised the price to both Imperial and other consumers. " Coal was such a daily necessity of life" said Councillor Hawthorn, "that the question arose whether such an important commodity should be the sport of a comparatively few individuals.— Even wealthy Cor- porations like their own were powerless to prevent a manipulation of prices to the detriment of the rate- payers" The community have the remedy in their own hands and this resolution moved by Councillor Stott points to a solution of these evils and it is on these lines alone that the general public can be adequately safe- guarded against unscrupulous coal-owners' exorbitant demands

Alderman Tinnion in adding his quota to the con- troversy said " they were met that evening to discuss business matters and not debating society pious opinions " and also declared "the matter had not been brought forward in a business way, and that the

41

5

present was not the proper time to discuss the question." Not the proper time indeed! Good gracious! Then why has Alderman Tinnion, during his many years' service as a prominent public man, neglected to draw attention to this extremely important question in a basineBB way and at (what appeared to him) the proper thee ? Let us for a moment take into consideration the advantage that would be socially gained through the municipalisation of even the mere retail distribution of coal. I notice that a committee of the Corporation have recently accepted a tender for best house coal at 14s. Der ton and, said Councillor Stott. "the contract was for a cnnparatively small supply." At the same time there are hundreds of working-class families in Warrington who pay at least one shilling per hundredweight (20s. per ton) for h aise coal. There- fore if a Corporation Committee can purchase house , coal six shillings per ton cheaper than private house- holders, why should not the Council undertake to I supply the inhabitants with the same quality of fuel at a lower price than they (the citizens) now pay to the private traders, and all profits made in the trans- action be applied to the relief of the rates? Just permit me to deal briefly with the oration of another " practical business politician " to wit Coun- cillor Rogers, who innocently asked the following comic questions : " Who was going to compensate the men who made their living out of the coal trade to-day, the coal- dealers and hawke:s ? Were they to go to the Work- house ? "

Dear me, John. that is " rich," and Councillor Rogers during his recent election campaign actually posed as an experienced public representwive. Does Mr. Rogers really believe that the numerous Town and County Councils now running their own trams despatched to various Workhouses all tbe drivers and conductors employed by the private companies who had previously controlled the tram service within their respective areas ? Surely the worthy Conn cillor is not afraid that his colleagues in the Warrington Town Council would (in the case of the coal supply becoming municipalised) send to the work- house every experienced coal hawker and dealer in the district, and then engage men who were mere " novices " in the business to administer the municipal coal supply. Excuse me, John, perhaps this gentle- man fears that coal when municipalised will tlwreby gain some peculiar qualities of a distinctly self-dis- tributive character and would thus entirely abolish the demand for human labour in connection with the coal trade ! One never knows ! ru conclusion, Sir, may I earnestly appeal to every intelligent reader to loyally support the proposed agitation in favour of a municipal coal supply which will when obtained thus humanise the industrial conditions of all the workers in the colliery and allied trades, also secure to the coal consumers of every class a cheaper supply than they at present receive and replace the existing chaotic, overlapping customs with greatly improved methods of systematic distribution.

DEHOS.

JUNIOR LIBERALS. "The Life of William Lloyd Garrison." This was the subject of a paper delivered by Mr. C. E. Parser to the Junior Liberals assembled at Gregory's Cafii on Monday evening. Mr. 11. W. Gueet presided. There was a fair attendance. Mr. PARKER said ha had chosen to speak to them of the life of William Lloyd Garrison (the man whose name will ever be associated with the freeing of American slaves) for two reasons, first, because he (Mr. Parker) recognize I the hero in the man, and secondly because he thought they Lwow too little of the great moral evolutions in other lands. Sketching Mr. Garrison's career, Mr. Parker said that on his father's side he spring from English stock, aud upon his mother's from an Ir:sh family that hailed from Limerick, but both families hid been settled in the American Continent for two geuerations before the year (1S05) in which William Lloyd Garrison was born. The childhood of Garrison was one long struggle against poverty and he manfulfy did his part in earning the daily bread. After repe ited efforts to settle him in some trade he was, at the age of thirteen, apprenticed to Ephreim W. Allen, editor of the " Newburyport Herald." At the expiration of his apprenticeship he left Mr. Allen and at the early age of twenty-ono became the editor of a paper entitled the "Free Press." Mr. Pars,r related how in this cepacity Girrison was able to encourage the early poetical efforts of Whittier. The first iodication he (Vlr. Parker) found of Garrison's interest in the slavery question sees an editorial note which he inserted in the " Free Press " commend- ing his readers to a poem on " Africa " written by a young lady. In 1828 Garrison understook the editor- ship of the " National Philanthropist " in Boston, a paper devoted to the suppressiou of intemperance. He made a memorable and stirring indictment of the slavery system. In this address be said ;—" I acknowledge that, immediate and complete emancipation is not desired "and went on to say "no rational man cherishes so wild a vision." But when he came to reflect upon the matter, he sew that in taking that I position his feet were on shifting sand. So long as he I -granted slavery the right to exist for a single moment, if human beings could be justly held in bondage One hour, they could be for days and weeks and years, indefinitely from generation to generation; but that the only way to deal with the system was to lay the axe at the root of the tree and demand immediate and unconditional emancipation. This conviction forced itself upon his mind during the five or six weeks which elapsed between the delivery of his address and his departure from Baltimore, to join Lundy with whom he had agreed to become joict editor of a paper entitled " The Genius of Universal Emancipation," called for short " The Genius." It was well to note that the early opponents of American slavery looked upon immedi- ate emancipation as an impossibility and that Garrison was the first to proclaim it. During Garrison's stay in Baltimore he saw much of the horrors of the slave trade. Mr. Parker recounted how Garrison suffered imprison- ment on account of the vigour with which Lie prspaeated his anti-slavery campaign, how on his rele-ise he addressed meetings and subsequently started a new paper called " The Liberator " devoted to the cause. Mr. Parker continued : " You youne men, look at this young man of twenty-four years of age and measure yourselves by him. See the odds against him, Church, State, wealth, society and law. For this youth to set himself to accuse the religious leaders of being false teachers who were deceiving others as well SS them- selves, to propose to uproot a system upon which the wealth of the country greatly depended, to attack the rights of property involving two millions of stools and many millions of dollars, to sweep away the whole system of laws in twenty States of the Union which recognised ownership in human flesh---surely never did anyone set out on such an apparently forlorn hope. But no human being ever set his head to a task with such high hope and such firm faith its did William Lloyd Garrison when he resolved in his heart that by the help of God he would overthrow slavery in his native land." Garrison through the "Liberator," gave the slave monopoly and indeed the country no rest and he soon gathered around him many adherents. With his pen he lashed out all round ; his words of righteous indignation were of the kind that burned and blistered. He bit straight from the shoulder and hit hard. Opposition was naturally aroused and measures taken to stamp out the "Liberator," The Corporation of Georgetown D.C. passed a law prohibiting any free person of colour from taking the " Liberator " from the Post Office under penalty of twenty dollars fine or thirty days imprison- ment and if fine and gaol fees were not paid, directing such person to be sold as a slave for four months. Garrison himself was threatened with violence and aksassination. But a protecting hand was over Garrison and his paper, and for twenty five years, years of stress and storm,the paper was continued until the truth hid conquered and the slave was free. Mr. Parker narrated some exciting *experiences in Garrison's career, an attempt being made on one occa- sion by a frenzied mob to lynch him. Concluding with a personal reminiscence, Mr. Parker said be well remembered the visit which the venerable reformer in his old age paid to his (the speaker's) own home in Scotland. Ile remembered taking part in unfurling the Stars and Stripes and the Union Jack upon the towers of their home. He remembered seeing his mother driving this hero up the avenue and whale Garrison saw the two flags which had been raised in his honour, he took off his het and acknowledged them as symbolising all that was good in the two nations and the fact that they then both waved over lands upon which human rights were acknowledged irrespective of colour or race. He (Mr. Parker) was bnt a lad then and his mother had told her children so much of the life of Garrison that some of them almost expected that, his would wear a halo round his brow. They found him a kindly old man whom neither the opposition of early days nor the flatter of later days had done anything to spoil. Might the inspiration of his life nerve the audience and him- self to higher purposes and worthier sacrifices. (Applause.) A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Parker, after which the CHAIRMAN said the subject had been a source of inspiration to them. All the great things were done by young men. The very best work had been done bs the very youngest men. The paper had been a great encouragement to them and he hoped they would derive a great deal of benefit from it. (Hear, bear.) Mr. PARKER said he was sorry be could not call him- self a young man. The relations between his own family and Garrison's caused additional interest in his paper. The life and character of Garrison was a very rough one and he treasured very greatly some letters he had from him to his mother. If the paper had interested them be was very pleased. (Hear, hear.) Next Monday evening Mr. England will give ma address.

THREE MEETINGS.

The puzzle made the time pass more quickly, and the speeches prosed away into silence in due course. The assemblage heaved a sigh of intense relief, and commenced the usual unceremonious scramble for refresh- ment. Terence stood aside, disregarding glances of invitation from various bright eyes, and watched people pressing and push- ing round the door. Only when the nurses began to file past did he turn his att•intion to things nearer at hand. The nurse he had noticed was sta.id;ng be- fore him, looking up into his face, while a dainty smile dimpled cheeks and lips. "It is !" she said. "I am sure it is. It iR Boy, is it not?" The old name took him back to the scenes of his early boyhood, and again he saw in his mind's eye the tiny white-robed figure of thi child he had loved so long ago. "Baby ! " he exclaimed, wondering. But she drew up her head with an odd re- semblance to the dignity of her toddling dais. They call me Nurse Barbara now," she said.

BY DOROTHY BAIRD,

Author of "My Beautiful Boy," "The Im- pertinence of Mr. Scroggins," "The Story of a Look," "The Girl with the Sweet Face," &c.

They met at a children's party. She was a tiny, toddling mite,with wide blue eyes, round, rosy cheeks, a cupid's bow mouth, and a mop e golden curls. She had a new dress for the occasion—creamy satin, very short in the waist, and very long in the skirt, and she was exceedingly proud of it. Naturally, she was the centre of admiration, and all the girls were clamouring for her to dap.:..e with them. But she refused with dignity; tile owner of a new satin dress, which almost touched the ground, could do with nothing less than a masculine partner. "No, no; want a boy," she reiterated to the crowding girls, and at last the Boy sum- moned up courage to push his way to where she stood. _

"And," he replied, laughing, "they call me Terence Sylvester." Then she laughed too, and the sound of that rippling, gurgling merriment seemed in a moment to hridge over the years that had slid into the past. "Sit down here," he said. "And tell me all that has happened to you since I saw you last. I will get you some tea when the crusb has cleared await a little." -- running mucn nas nappened," she said. "I am an orphan now, and poor—poor as the most ecclesiastical of mice. Nursing if to me bread and butter and everything else." "And where do you nurse?" "The hospital is in the slums, but we do outside nursing as well. The League is grow- ing fashionable, thanks to Lady Haviland." She is good to you?" "Oh, yes. She comes and fusses round now and then, and brings a lot of fashionable guests, whose trains sweep up the wards very nicely. Sometimes they condescend to in- spect some of the slum work. Not very often, thank goodness, for they are stupid and ignorant, and stir up feelings that are best left to sleep." "And she sometimes has you out to see her?"

" Dance with me, baby," he said, holding put his arms. She looked up at him, for he was very much taller than she, and for a moment his fate hung in the balance. Perhaps it was the light on his shining red-gold hair, or the sweet smile on his pale, sensitive face that took her fancy, but she smiled suddenly, and holding up her tiny hands to him burst into a low, gurgling laugh. And so they went off together, while friends and mothers watched, witn amused smiles on their lips. He was so tall and lanky—as a delicate boy will often be in his early teens, when he is working hard for a scholarship--T and she so round, and soft, and rosy. And he handled her tenderly enough. At first he had felt gaunt and awkward, but the cling of the tiny lingers thrilled him suddenly, remind- him of a little sister who had gone back to God when she was about this baby's age. And his tenderness called to the feminine in tier, and she would not leave him, but clung to him all the evening. He was contented -enough, for he was shy and sensitive, and knew very few people in the place as yet, for they had but lately come into the district. Later on there were crackers., Baby's dis- closed a large silvery brooch, on which was inscribed "I'll be your sweetheart." "What is sweetheart?" she asked, when someone read the motto.

"Yes. She likes us to come to these func- tions in our spare time: instead of taking a run out into the country. I suppose she thinks it is good for us to have a glimpse of high .life." He laughed. " You don't take to high life?" he asked. She made a moue of disgust.

"It's what Boy is to you," said someone vvickedly, and she turned a contemplative eye tpon his blushing face. "Me like sweethearts !" she said, boldly, -and the laugh which followed nearly brought her to tear.

`-`—lt is so sufiy and so pushing," Phe said. "If a meeting in the East End was conclucteo like this there would be letters to the paper about it. 13-,lt perhaps it is only because lam not used to it that I feel like this."

She cried again when she had to go and leave the Boy behind, clinging to him with tightly clutching fingers. and only half com- forted when they promised that she would often see him again. But the promise was never kept. Once, the day when first she was taken to church, she saw him across the aisle, and the dignity and silence which had been laboriously impressed upon her left her suddenly. Boy, boy !" she shrilled, stretching out her hands towards him, and starting to toddle down the pew. But he signed to her to be silent, while anxious maternal hands pulled her back, and plumped her down so that she could not see him. Now and then she would wriggle till she obtained a glimpse of him, sedulously copying whatever she saw him do, so that she really behaved better for the latter half of the service. They smiled at one another as they came out, but at the porch she was seized by a waiting nurse and hurried home, and so they never spoke. Very soon after that her parents lost their money, and left the neighbourhood, to live down their poverty in a poorer quarter, so that the Baby and the Boy did not meet any more. But that was how it began.

And so they chatted and filled up the gaps in the story of their friendship. And ‘vhile he heard of her work and her dear poor people, she learnt of his pictures. and his studio, and his long sketching expeditions.

"You are famous?" she asked. looking tip at him, and thinking bow handsome he had grown. He had lost his gawky loch. though he was still very tall. His early delicacy had left its mark on his face. but the pale com- plexion went well with the burnished hair. and the clear grey eyes were merry and kindly. The sensitive lips were grave and sad when at rest, but when they smiled the expression was very sweet. "You are popular t," she added to herself. "No man can smile like that and not win hearts to himself."

They saw a good deal of one another in the months that, followed—Terence took care of that—and for a time .she enjoyed his friendship to the full. Then he re-introduced her to his family, and she noticed that his mother received her coldly, and did not echo his invitation to the house. That opea her eyes, and she understood all in a UM- ment why his very evident popularity had power to hurt her. -41 e loved him just as sue had loved him in the old days, only with the force of a woman, instead of the innocence of a child, and she saw that a gulf was placed between them, that yawning guf which society says neither man nor woman must pass. He was rich, brilliant, famous, and held a goo-I position in the world. He must make a mar- riage suitable to that position; there were plenty of great ladies who loved him as much as she. She was poor and insignificant, and she had the gift which is fatal in the hands of poverty and insignificance, the gift a beauty. She was not vain, but her common sense told her that her presence was a dang -r to him. She was not of the sentimentat type of woman, who believes that a love matcl,, matter what unpleasantness and happiness. crushing bitterness it may bring, is the best thing that can happen to a man. She was unselfish enough to wish only what was good for him, and she would not be the one to spoil his career, estrange him from his family, and make enemies of his friends. She told herself that if she went away he would speedily forget her, as she thought he had forgotten her before, and so, beyond a passing irritation, she would cause him no trouble, and all would go well with him, while for herself it did not much matter.

It was a broiling summer afternoon. Out- side a pitiless sun glared on white roadway and dusty trees, on baking houses and droop- ing window box flowers. Inside the light was subdued by sun-blinds and rose-coloured cur- tains, the air heavy with hothouse flowers, so heavy that the fashionable crowd who panted on the stairways and scrambled for chairs in the spacious reception rooms were almost tempted to wish that Lady Haviland were not quite so famous for her success in horti- cL!cure.

The occasion was an "At home," with a charitable object, for Lady Haviland, vainly trying to reconcile a frivolous nature with Puritan ancestry and tradition, would allow no gaiety in her house unless it could be illumined by a halo of religious intention. The favoured charity that afternoon was a nursing league in which Lady Haviland took special interest. She had spared no pains to snake the function a success, and she regarded the crowd, pushing and elbowing its way into her rooms, with an expression of extreme coMplacency. There are some people who are perfectly happy if they can cram three hundred people of title and distinction into a space calculated to accommodate a hundred with comfort, and a hundred and fifty with care and arrangement. Lady Haviland was one of these. The fact that the house was overcrowded almost to suffocation, and that at least a dozen eminent physicians and clergymen graced her platform, and that three Dukes would speak the necessary words for passing a vote of thanks to her and other organisers, filled her soul with joy. But these same facts filled the souls of her guests with dismay. It was so very hot and stuffy, and the speeches were so exceedingly prosy, that not a few wished they could flee away and be at rest. Some made a pretence of listening, but they were in the minority— most were openly bored.

And so, without saying anything to anybody, she offered herself to one of the missionary societies, and, being strong, and bright, and plucky, she was readily accepted. And she was soon sent to a post of considerable im- portance, for her knowledge of nursing made her _valuable on the mission field.

Thus it happened that she slipped once more out of Terence Sylvester's life. He made many efforts to trace• her, but they were in vain, and at last he almost gave up hope of ever seeing her again. But that was the intermediate stage.

Terence 'Sylvester was one of the latter. He fidgeted as much as politeness allowed. and frequently consulted his watch, under Cover of his coat. Presently he began to amuse himself by studying his fellow-victims, storing up impressions for further use, now and then sketching in a face, with a few bold touches on the margin of the pamphlet about the organisation which had been given him. And so his eye strayed to the place where a contingent of nurses from the League, grey. cloaked and bonneted, sat together below" the platform. They were mostly pretty, he de- cided. A soft, pale grey is a colour that suits most faces, and a nurse's dress is eminently becoming. There was a nurse at the end of the row nearest to him who was even prettier than the rest—blue-eyed, gold m- haired, and rosy-cheeked. Her face struck him as being somehow familiar, but he could put no name to it. Think as be would_ he

They met again, however, and once more it was at a party. Very different was it, how- ever, from either of the others which had brought them together before. Here there was very little gaiety, and no attempt at fine dressing. A few officers in khaki, some nurses, and a meagre collection of thin and harassed- looking civilians were gathered in one of the wards of the little hospital, trying to while away an hour or so of pain for the sufferers, and of weary anxiety for themselves by a little music. But it seemed as if the music brought more pain than joy, for songs of happier days called tears to many eyes, and the sounds from the beleaguered town formed but dreary accompaniment. Terence Sylvester had arrived here only a month ago, meaning to stay but one night, and then push on towards the Highlands of Central Asia. He had grown restless when he found his efforts to trace Nurse Barbara were of no avail. and had commenced a' series

CI nuerrngs tar ana wrae over the world, painting those pictures which the nation now guards so jealously as some of her most price- less treasures. He had seen evidences of dis- turbance as he came through the country, but had apprehended nothing serious. China was always more or less disturbed, and if the natives should rise, a few shots would soon settle matters.

But he had barely been twelve hours in the place before he found himself shut in. Nanikou was little more than a mission station, held, for some inscrutable purpose, by the British Empire, who kept a small gar- rison within its walls. The little force was totally inadequate to hold the town success- fully against the hordes of malignant China- men who poured in from the hostile towns and villages for miles round. Escape was impossible, because of the disaffected state of the whole district; they could only do the best they could with their scant garrison, augmented by all the civilians and a few natives connected with the mission, and wait for help, which, from the position of the place, must necessarily be long delayed. So far they had held out successfully, but every day the supplies grew scantier, the wards of the improvise hospital at the mission station more crowded. All suffered more or less from the cramped conditions of life, and the crushing sense of despair, arising from the knowledge that it was now but a matter of days, weighed down every spirit. It was io the hope of dispelling the depression that Terence sylvester had suggested some mild amusement, to keep thoughts from dwelling too much upon the horrors which lay in the near future. He arranged it all, cheering many a drooping spirit by his brightness, his unfailing hopefulness, and courage.

For -himself, he feared death not at all. Life had been disappointing to him, and if his call came he would go gladly; but the thought of the women and children in the town falling into the hands of the cruel, yell- ing savages haunted him. He could see no way out for them, and he had much ado to keep outwardly cheerful. Then, when he went to the long, low, hos- pital ward that night, and saw a familiar small grey figure moving among the convales- cents, it seemed as if his heart stood still. He had thought to be so glad to see har again, and now the joy was swallowed up in one great sickening fear. The woman he loved was here, almost defenceless, while outsid yelled a mob notorious throughout the world for its hideous cruelty. She seemed to feel his gaze upon her, for suddenly she looked up,and her bright, whole- hearted smile spread over her features. "So you have found me, after all," she said. •

For a moment he was speechless. Wild plans for eseape, for saving her, surged through his brain. "I have looked long," he said at last. "And now—would to heaven I had not found

you ! " A sudden fear shot across her face. There is no hope?." she asked.

"None. Help is still four days off. Any moment now they may break through the walls. Of course, we shall fight step by step, bat it jII merely be a matter of time."

The horror of the thought shone through her eyes for an instant, then she was brave end smiling again. "We shall die together," she said, very ;oftly. _ _

"That comforts you?" he

P His face lit up. ulcer, eagerly. Barbara nodded. "It is better than a long life apart," she said, and there was a little sobbing catch in her voice, which told him all the truth. He held her trembling hand in his for a moment, and then they separated, Bar- bara to her duties, Terence to keep things going as merrily as might be. It was towards the end of the evening that the alarm sounded. To those who understood, sounds from without told the fell news long before the orders came. Every step was being contested, but the last stand was to be made at the mission. That had been pre- arranged, and they must hold themselves pre- pared. Quickly and quietly arms and ammuni- tion were given out, and men posted at every door and window, while women, and even children, undertook the work of re-loading. And so they waited, almost in darkness, for the death which now lay so. close ahead.

At the first alarm Ist'urse Barbara came to Terence's side.

"It is a question of minutes now," she said, with a wan little smile. He nodded, watching her deft fingers lay- ing the cartridges in order to her hand,so that she might not fumble in the darkness. "I am glad I saw you again—first," she said, and her voice trembled.

His arm went round her waist, and he drew her to him unresisting. "It is—worth dying for," he whispered, hoarsely. A lurid light flickered against the wails; the work of destruction had begun. Nurse Barbara stood up at her post. " Look," she said, pointing to a single car- tridge put apart from the rest. "That is for me. You promise?" And with white lips Terence promised. Another instant and the crowd was upon them. Terence and Nurse Barbara worked with the silent energy. of despair. It seemed a horrible nightmare of fire, and yells, and horrible demon faces, hut it did not last long. As Barbara had said, it was merely a mr.Atter of minutes. To Terence it seemed but a mo- ment before she put the revolver into his hand. "Shoot five," the said, in a clear, low voice. "But the sixth is mine. It is the last."

And so he turned. She wns kneeling now, clasping his left hand in both her own. " Quick !" she whispered. " You will come soon !"

Some instinct gave him pause. The dim, flickering, red light shewed her kneeling figure but vaguely, but he could see that her eyes were closed, and that her lips moved silently. Outside the winaow fantastic figures moved and struggled against the flame-lit sky, shots from other windows still kept the crowd at bay, but it could not last long. Every moment the figures crept nearer, till the evil, yellow faces could be distinguished clearly, all wreathed with demon smiles of hate and revenge. Then he glanced back round the room. On the long rows of white beds the sufferers lay very still, waiting, as they had waited for many days, for death, which was now to come quickly and sud- denly, and not, as they had expected, by long, lingering degrees. The little groups around the windows were thinning, gradually and surely, and in most eases awn were Ehooting over the dead bodies of fallen com- rades. And still at his feet knelt Nurse Bar- bara, her hands clasped, her lips still moving. Suddenly she opened her eyes, and looked up at him. " How long you are," she said. " Please—"

He looked down at her sadly, and shook his head.

"But you promised," she cried. "I trusted you." She was clasping his hands, looking up at him with piteous pleading in her eyes. For answer be closed and barred the shutters of the window beside \ him, Other windows were being closed and barred tte well.

"A few minutes more," he said, quietly. "We have had so few, and the years have been long." "But there is eternity in front," she re- plied. But she pleaded no more, only knelt beside him, clinging closely to his hand. Louder and louder surged the din outside, and the smell of burning came , strong upon the breeze.

"It does seem hard, doesn't it," she whis• pered, "that we should have to die so soon— and yet, if it had been different, I couldn't have said to you what I have said; I couldn't have knelt like this; I couldn't have kissed your hand like this—and this !" He put his arms round her and drew her close. - _

• "It's only the poor - fertows at • 'tile gate:, he said. "A momentary success." "They must have died long ago 1" she exclaimed. "And listen ! There it is again; nearer, louder."

"I would never have parted from you again," he said. "Never ! Never !" She nestled to him silently. Here, in the very shadow of death, she could not argue with him, but she felt that death was giving her what life would never have given. Suddenly she lifted her head. "Hark !" she exclaimed.

Then be understood that report had lied, andlhat help was at the very door. Louder and louder came the welcome sound, while the demon rabble broke, and fled hither and thither in the darkness. For a time he could not speak, feeling surged too high within him. He could only watch Nurse Barbara as sheyPeered through a chink in the shutter. "I thought help was four days off," she said, and her voice was dull, liLe the voice of one who speaks in a dream. Terence eaught her as she swayed. "I will never let you go again," ho said. "Never I Never '

Clear above the yells of the mob came the faint echo of -a cheer. For a moment Terence's heart leaped in his bosom with a wild burst of hcau4...but. he knew. that it waY vain.

Years afterwards Terence and Darin:re, Syl- vester visited the mission at Nanikou again. "It was lihe an evil dream," said Darbara, shuddering a little as she looked at the win- dow they had defended. "And yet I wouldn't be without the memory," replied Terence, dreamily. Their eyes met. '`lsior would I," silo answered softly., MIR END.I

The Examiner, Saturlay January 25, 1908.

ST, AUSTIN'S WARD ELECTION.

MR. BROADHURST RETURNED.

After a week's keen campaign, the bye-election in St. Austin's Ward on Monday resulted in the return of Mr. James Broadhurst (C) who defeated Mr. John Maginn (I.) by 200 votes. During the greater part of the day fog enveloped the town and made electioneering anything but pleasant. The workers on each side entered into the contest with enthusiasm and during the dinner hour and in the evening polling was brisk. There was a plentiful display of mural literature. Mr. Maginn appealing for support mainly on the ground of his independence and the fact that in his ronnicipal work be would not be tied to any party, political or religious. He also advanced several sciiemes for the improvement of the town and the ward. The Conserva- tives fought the election entirely on political lines and endeavoured to make capital out of the education question.

.„ -- PUBLIC DECLARATION. Notwithstanding the cold and foggy weather there was a large crowd to bear the declaration of the result from the Town Hall steps. COOIIOMOT BROADHURST, who was cheered, thanked the electors for what they bad done for him, also those who had worked so hard for him from the highest to the men be loved best—the workingmen who had worked without any compensation. They would under- stand that it was very difficult for one in his position to say what be would like to say, hut he could certainly sympathize with Mr. Maginn, who had treated him as a perfect gentleman. (Cheers.) Mr. MA ors said it was unnecessary for him to tell the audience that be was disappointed with the result of the election. He bad, however, one feeling of pleasure, and that that was the first time in the four elections he bail fought that be had met a thorough workingman who understood what it was to work with his hands as well as with his head. With the exception of a few spars, common on omis- sions like that, he had nothing to complain of except of course. that be was not in the proud position Mr. Broadlanrst now held. In his oninion they bad not conferred the honour npou the best man--(laughter)— but he must congratulate the electors on conferring it on the second best man. He thanked the 331 persons who had voted for hint, and also the 531 who had been manly and courageous enough to vote against him. He despised those weak-kneed creatures who had a vote and yet had not the manliness to use it. With reference to the position be took up some six years ago, be maintained still that a man who wished to represent the ratepayers should be independent and free from all cliques and political or social parties. He had been defeated several times, but he still believed he was right, although be was afraid be was a little before bis time. Some day they would recognize that fact, but he would then be too old to fight the battle. The crowd then dispersed. At the Conservative Club there was much jubilation and numerous speeches were delivered.

A WARNING OF INTEREST TO THE PUBLIC

To what a grievious extent the public suffers through the prevalence of imitations and substitutions but few of our readers can appreciate, even though they are themselves frequently victimised. The evil is greatest when the article imitated is either a food or a medicine for in this case the health of the victim suffers as well as his pocket. Amongst the multitude of advertised articles that nre imitated there is hardly one that is not manufactured in some special manner or by some secret process that makes their successful imitation impossible. Take as one instance of this the well- known and—largely advertised Angler's Emulsion. This preparation is different to all other emulsions, because It is made with petroleum specially purified by a secret process known only to the proprietors. No other chemist can obtain this special petroleum ; how then can he imitate it? To be sure, any chemist can make an emulsion of petroleum, but it will be very different and very inferior to Angler's, as every honest chemist will admit. Here we have an original preparation of undoubted merit that is endorsed by the medical profession, and used all over the world ; and yet many persons are induced, to their own detriment, to buy a cheap imitation on the times worn plea that it is" just as good." As regards the example we have given (Angier's Emulsion), there is no excuse for being deceived, for the proprietors (The Angier Chemical Company, Ltd., 32, Snow Hill, London E.C.), oiler to send a free sample on receipt of a small sum for postage. The Angier Chemical Co.'s advertise- ments ought to convince readers of the wisdom of first sending for a free sample of the original before trying an imitation, and thus themselves be able to judge of the superior qualities of the advertised article.

A SHORT WAY WITH DEBTORS.

On Saturday, at Runcorn, William Cressey, fish- dealer, was sentenced to one month's hard labour for assaulting a coloured seaman named George Ernest Thomas, who had his bead swathed in bandages. Thomas owed Crassey Ils, and promised to give him money at the post office. Thomas failed to get the money there, and on coming outside Crersey felled him with 'short bludgeon, inflicting a serious wound over the eye and tendering him insensiblo.

UNCLE NED.

"GIVE US A FLOWER, SISTER."

One of our nurses was hurrying along in a ilistrict one day with sonic flowers which she was taking to her patients, when a man etand- ing on the pavement asked her for one of them. Hurriedly she took a little bunch and handed it to him. Some two or three years afterwards she went into a house where some men were at work. "You don't remember me, sister," said one of them. "No, I'm afraid I don't," said the sister.

"Well, do you remember a man asking you for some flowers one flay in the street " "Yes—yes, I remember now." "Well, I thought you might like to know what became of them. I took them flowers to a mate of mine in the 'orspital, and, sister, he died with them flowers in his ,hand. He was glad to have 'em." GERTIE SCRIVEN.

A FUNNY LITTLE CUSTOMER.

"A pennyworth of what, my dear?" ex- claimed old Seabright. "A penn.'orth of de- lightfulness, if you please," repeated the child. "Delightfulness!" and the old man's face as- sumed a puzzled expression. The little girl stood gravely at the counter, wonderiug at the old man's peculiar behaviour. "Delightfulness! delightfulness! I have never been asked for that before. Do you mean these?" said the old man, showing his little customer a bottle of honeydrops. "No, sir," replied the little girl, shaking her curly head energetically. "Is this it?" inquired he, showing a tablet of Turkish Delight. Oh, yes, that is delightfulness," exclaimed the little girl, and, putting down her penny, she departed with her little pur- chase. EDGAR MARTIN.

NIGHT. Night is the time for rest; How sweet! when laboull close, To gather round an ac ling breast

The curtain of repose; Stretch the tired limbs and lay the head Upon our own delightful bedl Night is the time for dreams; The gay romance of life, When truth that is, and truth that seems,

Blend in fantastic strife; Ah i'visions less beguiling far • Than waking dreams by daylight are. Night is the time for foil; To plough the classic field, Intent to find the buried spoil,

Its wealthy furrows yield; Till all is ours that sages taught, That poets sang or heroes wrought. Night is the time to weep;

To wet with unseen tears Those graves of memory where sleep The joys of other years; Hopes the.* were angels in their birth, But perished young, like things of earth. Night is the time for care; Brooding on hours mis-spent, To see the spectre of despair e- Come to our lonely tent; Like Brutus, midtst his slumbering host, Startled by CEesar's stalwart, ghost. Night is the time to pray; Our Saviour oft withdrew To desert mountains far away; So will His followers do: Steal from the throng to haunts untrod, And hold communion there with God. Night is the time for death, When all around is peace, Calmly to yield the weary breath, From sin and suffering cease; Think, of Heaven's bliss, and give the sign To parting friends--such death be mine! IIINDLE

A man had several sons who were such quarrelsome boys that they could never agree with each other. So he called them together, and had a bundle of sticks brought. c;w "Now, boys," said their father, "take this bundle, and let me see which of you can break it." One after the other tried his hardest, but the sticks were so closely bound together that they would not break. Then the father untied the bundle, and gave one single stick to each boy, telling him to break it.

"Oh, that is quite easy," said they. "You see, my boys," said their father, "you ape like the bundle of sticks. 'While you keep together and agree, no one can hurt you; when you fall apart, any one may do you harm."

ALL CAN DO SOMETHING. Every little flower cin.'t blossom Like the blushing rose, Or be like the stately lily That eo lovely growe. But each tender little flowerlet

Can have light and grace, Grow within its little corner, Sweeten its own place. All the birds like soaring skylark Cannot reach the sky, Nor at '\morn rise up to heaven— All can't mount so high. But each tiny little songster Has its bough or tree,

Where it sings all through the summer, Songs to you and me. And each little lad or maiden Can't expect, you see, To be great and wise and learned, Yet each one may be Sweet, as all the flowers have sweetness; Like the little bird, Help to make the work more pleasant With a song or word.

R. WRBURTON.

THE HORSE AND LOADEff ASS.

The ass had both his baskets full, besides a heavy sack laid across his back, while his com- panion, the horse, trotted gaily along with nothing to carry. The ass asked him if he would kindly take a part of the load, but the ill-natured horse threw up his head and re- fused. Presently the heavy-laden ass fell slown and died, and the master, taking up the harden, put it upon the horse, and then, lifting up the poor ass, put him upon the horse's back also.

"I wish I had not been so ill-natured," said the horse. "If I had not refused to carry part of the weight, I should not now have to carry the whole." GWENNIE KIVELL.

A SILLY BOY.

A gentleman once asked a boy what was the matter with him, when he replied, "I am silly, sir." "Then," said the gentleman, "here is sixpence, for you." Then, to the gentleman's astonishment, the boy said, "I have got a brother at home who is silly, too." "Then," said the gentleman, meaning to get the sixpence back, "give me the sixpence, and I will give you a shilling," "No," said the boy, "I'm not so silly as air that, sir."

FLOSSIE C. NEALE.

PRACTICAL ENGINEERING.

Jones owns a small motor-car. He has no chauffeur, and every time he goes out a break- down occurs. No wonder. "Hello!" he ex- claimed, on meeting a friend at the club the other day. "I've been spending a profitable afternoon." "Have you?" said the friend. "How?" "Why, I took the engine of my car all apart to see how it really worked." "Great Scot, old man," exclaimed the friend, realising Jones's impracticability, "I hope you were careful not to mislay any of the parts." "No fear!" answered the expert engineer. "Why when I put it together again I had nearly half a dozen pieces over!" GISH* FLETCHER.

SARAH'S SURPRISE.

- _ There was once a little girl whose name was. Sarah. She was very fond of surprising people. Once, when she was playing hide-and-seek with her brother, she hid herself in a large box, and drew clown the lid. She stayed in the box for a long time waiting for her brother to come. She thought when he came she would juinp up and, frighten him. She tried to raise the lid, but found she could not do so. It was fast, and she could not get out. She became so frightened that she screamed until her mother. came to her rescue. Sarah never again hid her- self in a box. Wishing you a prosperous New Year: WINNIE FRANKS.
