THAT OLD WINTER COUGH.

Patients Cure makes her Doctor now prescribe PEPS to Others.

So strikingly successful were Peps in curing the obstinate cough of Mrs. C. M. Pyle, of 13, Carlingford Road, Hampstead, London, N.W., that her doctor is now recommending this wonderful medicine to his other patients.

Peps differ entirely from 'ordinary cough mixtures, especially the old-fashioned liquid mixtures and that Peps are a vastly superior invention is shown by the fact that the cough Mrs. Pyle so easily cured, lead for yearA resixted every other known form of treatment, including a voyage to Australia and back. " I had more chest trouble in the Colonies," says Mrs. Pyle, " and as soon as I reached London again, the old winter cough returned worse than ever. The cough was a dreadful utrangling rough and my breathing at times became quite difficult. Peps were the only remedy which brought me genuine relief amongst the many I tried. The result of takin,-, the first few tablets was so wonderful that I at once sent some Peps to my daughter, and to several friends. Even my doctor is now recommending them to his patients. " The last think as I lie down for the night, I put a Peps tablet on my tongue. My breathing is at once relieved. If 1 cough at all it is just a soft clearing of the throat, so different from the dreadfnl strangling fits of coughing I had to endure before I resorted to Peps. As I am now 75 years of age, and have been subject to this winter cough 'for quite half my lifetime, the result achieved by Peps in my case is certainly wonderful."

Peps are the World's greatest breathe-able cough and cold medicine, and being free from opium, chloral and all other drowsy, nerve- depressing drugs, are greatly valued by elderly people and young children. See that the word PEPS is on every box. Sold by all chemists at is. 11d. and 2s. 9d. (three times the number in the 2s. 9d. size).

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

TOAD VENOM.

The poisonous secretions from two varie- ties of toads—Bufo vulgaris and B. viridis— have been obtained by injecting a solution of barium chloride or stimulating the skin by means of an induction coil, either of which methods causes the glands to discluirg,e the fluid. It js a milk-white juice, which, ex- ternally applied, has a strong irritant action upon the mucous membrane and eausca burn- ing of the eye, the effects disappearing snot- a few hours. Introduction of the venom into the .blood kills dogs within an hour, and in the case of frogs, produces convulsions, end- ing in paralysis and death. Alcohol and opiates act as antidotes to the poison. Toads are not susceptible to the poisonous secre- tions of closely allied species, though they are so to the venom of the salamander and triton.

HIGH TEMPERATUHES.

High temperatures now play an important part in our daily work, and it is interesting to note the following actual temperatures of familiar objects and operations, as deter- mined by a new optical pyrometer. Porcelain furnace, 2,498 deg. Palm; glass furnace, 2,552c1eg. ; open hearth steel, 2,795 deg.; melted platinum, 34i36deg.; incandescent lamp, 3,272 deg.; arc lamp, 7,410 deg.; the sun, 13,713 deg.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
FERTILISING WASTE LAND.

The possibilities of certain grasses being utilised for the purpose of fertilising, and thereby reclaiming for cultivation, waste stretches such as sand dunes, has been strik- ingly demonstrated upon King Island, which is situated between the coasts of Tasmania and the Australian mainland. This island has always been an arid waste of sand and other non-arable soil. Some few years ago, however, a vessel was wrecked off the island, and when broken up under the force of the waves a number of the sailors' mattresses, which were stuffed with yellow-flowered clover—a kind of grass—were washed ashore. A certain quantity of seed was contained among the stuffing, and in due course these took root, and, owing to their prolific growth, in the space of a few years covered the sandy stretches with rich verdure. It is a long-estab- lished fact that clover and other leguminous plants have the peculiar capacity of fertilis- ing a waste soil, owing principally to the ac- tion of bacteria, thereby enabling the plants to draw nitrogen directly from the atmo- sphere. In the case of King Island, owing to the properties of this yellow-flowered cltver, what was previously a waste stretch of sand is now one of the richest grazing districts in the Australian continent. The growth of the plant completely changes the character and colour,of the soil from a dirty white to a rich dark brown or black loamy nature.

A CHEMICAL PUZZLE.

The following experiment, says M. Gustave Michaud, of the Costa Rica State College, is easily performed, even by those- who have had little or no previous chemical training. It gives an idea of the infinite diversity of chemical and physical changes which one and the same reagent may induce in other sub- stances.

Fourteen glasses, arranged in seven pairs, are brought before the spectators, who are re- quested to examine them and 'their contents. The experimentor then pours one and the same liquid into the fourteen glasses. The following contradictory results become at once apparent: Substance contented in glass A becomes so hot that the glass cannot be kept in the hand. Substance contained in glass Al becomes so cold- that frost promptly collects outside of the glass. Liquid contained in glass B was blue; it becomes colourless. Liquid contained in glass B' was colourless; it becomes blue.

Glass V contained a clear liquid which be- comes muddy. Glass Co contained a muddy liquid• which becomes clear. If a lighted match be introduced into glass D, several inches above the liquid, noisy flames are seen to spring in every direction. If the same experiment is made in the sanbe manner with glass the match is quietly but immediately extinguished. Liquid in glass E had a burning, suffocating smell; it becomes odourless. Liquid in glass E' was odourless; it acquires such an offensive smell that it becomes necessary to carry it outside. Liquid in glass F was red; it becomes blue. Liquid in glass Ff was blue; it becomes red. Glass G contained a solid substance which becomes liquid. Glass 0' contained a clear liquid which becomes instantaneously solid

===== 0002601_19080125_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXPLANATION.

Every transformation is the result of the action of common hydrochloric acid over some chemical. Here is the nature and pro- portions of these; capacity of glasses being supposed to be about one pint. Glass A contains 50 grammes of sodium hydrate dissolved in 100 cubic centimetres of sat. This solution fills about one-half of the glass. During the experiment the other half must be entirely filled with hydrochloric acid. This should be poured slowly while the liquid is agitated with a glass or wooden fi tick. The last additions of acid cause the liquid to boil. The glass will then be found to contain com- mon table salt mixed with an excess either of acid or of sodium hydrate. Glass A' is filled with sodium sulphate in small crystals, such as is sold by pharmacists to be taken inter- nally. Enough hydrochloric acid must be poured in to cover the salt. Temperature goes at once much below 32deg. Pahl% Cold becomes still more intense if the mixture he agitated. Three-quarters of glass B are filled with water; then one decigranune of copper sul- phate is dissolved in it. Ammonia is added in small portions until an intense blue colour appears. The addition of hydrochloric acid will cause it to vanish. A solution of two centigrammes of potassium ferricyanide in 100 cubic centimetres of water is made in glass is", and in this another solution of three centigrammes of ferrous sulphate in 100 cubic centimetres of water is poured. A little ammonia is then added until the beauti- ful blue colour vanishes. Hydrochloric acid will cause it to reappear instantaneously. Glass C contains the ordinary solution of lead acetate sold by druggists for local or ex- ternal application. Hydrochloric acid forms in it a dense precipitate of lead chloride. Three-quarters of glass C" are filled with water to which about a quarter of a teaspoon- ful of slaked lime, free from coarse particles, i§ added. Calcium chloride, resulting from the action of hydrochloric acid on lime, is ex- ceedingly soluble in water. . . _ _

A fe-w' pieces of zinc are deposited in glass D. Bubbles of hydrogen will set noisily on fire when a match is introduced into the glass. The experiment is free from danger so long as no attempt-is made to close the glass. One-third of glass D' is filled with wood ashes with enough water to make a thin paste. Car- bon dioxide is the gas which promptly extin- guishes the match. A hundred cubic centi- metres of water, fifty cubic centimetres of ammonia, and enough litmus solution to give the liquid a bluish tint are poured into glass E. During the experiment, hydrochloric acid is added, little by little, until the bluish colour suddenly becomes reddish. The odour will then be found to have vanished.

One-third of glass E' is filled with water, and eight grammes of powdered iron sulphide are thrown into it. Hydrochloric acid will generate hydrogen sulphide in the glass. This gas is the active agent found in some mineral waters, to which is gives a characteristic smell of putrefied eggs. It is poisonous, hut the amount evolved during the experiment is small, and the smell is so strong that it makes the air apparently irrespirable long be- fore there is any danger. It should not, how- ever, be smelt directly over the glass, nor should the glass remain in the room longer than is necessary to detect the odour: Glass F contains the same liquid as used in glass B. with the athlition of one centigramme of aniline red scarlet. Glass I," contains the ordinary solution of blue litmus. Calcined magnesia is the solid substance which fills about one-third of glass a, and the ordinary syrupy soluble class or sodium sili-

Warrington Win Well at Swinton.

Warrington took a strong team to Swinton on Satur- day to play the return match with the `• Lions " and had no difficulty in heating them soundly for the second time this season. Beautifully fine weather prevailed and about 4,000 spectators assembled., Ssvinton bad their old forwsrd"L Preston, back again. He had only been re-transferred from Warrington the same week. Teams and officials :

Swinton—Townson, back; Wallwork, Hopwood, Walsh and Griffin, three-quarter backs ; Morgan and Cunningham. half-backs; Simister, Barnett, Lloyd, Rickers, J. Preston and W. Preston:forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back ; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son and Taylor, three-quarter backs; Brooks and Jenkins, half-backs ; A. E. Boardman, E Boardman Thomas, Belton, Heath and Harmer, forwards. Referee, Mr. E. 14. Smirk, Preston. Touch judges, Messrs. H. Davies, Flixton, and T. Melledew, Rochdale. Hornets.

I —Wewrr.in.gton lost the toss, and Thomas started, exchanges between Brooks and Wallwork witnessing the Swintonian faulty at fielding, and the visitors were at once on the aggressive. Warrington early claimed an advantage forward, and a penalty against Barnett was , well utilized by Thomas. Eventually the Warrington 'tacks got their passing machinery working, and Brooks and Jenkins baffling the opposition, Isherwood fed Fish, who jumped over Townson and scored finely. The try was made in a favourable position, and Thomas had little difficulty in adding the goal. Swinton pressed after this but there was little sting in the attack, and Tilley lad nob much difficulty in forcing the locals back to their quarters. The game took an even turn, several smart forward rushes more than counter- balancing the neat passing of the Warrington rear men. An injury to Thomas delayed the game slightly, and Swinton pressed a little and appeared to have a chance of drawing level when Tilley stumbled. The Warrington custodian, however, effected a fine recovery, and punted well down the field. Wallwork made a smart run, and appeared dangerous until Taylor arrested progress. Fish made another capital effort, but superior numbers spoiled his chance of crossing. Wild passing by the home backs gave Dickenson a chance, and the Swinton line was hotly assailed as the result of a smart pass to Taylor. Warrington continued to apply the pressure, and after Jenkins had been pulled up on the line the spectators were roused to a high pitch of enthu- siasm, when Brooks and Jenkins combined, and the three-quarter line again demonstrating their superiority, Fish put OD the finishing touch to a really brilliant movement. Thomas failed at goal, and at half-time the visitors led by one goal two tries (eight points) to nil.

On resuming Warrington were soon attacking and after Fish had plhced his side in a good position and Barnett had relieved, the superiority of the leaders was once more demonstrated when the backs got passing, and again the ball travelled in the direction of Fish who cleverly eluded several of the visitor, and scored his third try. The home men could not make any headway against the superior tactics of the opposition, and after Griffin had palpably fouled the Warrington half-back, and Thomas had placed a goal, the spectators were treated to another fine effort by Fish, who notched his fourth try. The game *arely escaped the home quarters, and near the close Taylor put on the finishing touch to a round of passing, and Thomas made a capital attempt to land the goal, the ball striking the post. Warrington alwacs held the upper hand, and both fore and aft they proved them- selves immeasurably the superior team. Result : Warrington, three goals five tries (21 points), Swinton nil.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCASHIRE SHIELD COMPETITION. WARRINGTON " A " V. EGERTON.

By arrangement this match, fixed to be played at Egerton, took place at Wilderspool on Saturday. There was a crowd of quite 4,000, the attraction being Watkin, the Abertillery three-quarter, who made his first appearance with the Warrington club. The visitor's were early compelled to adopt the defensive, and after Lloyd had effected a capital lUD, Forster fielded from play and scored near the corner. The same player again became prominent and scored near the posts to add the goal. Egerton then lost their full back through injury. Watkin next put on the finishing touch to a capital round of passing, whilst a forward rush over- came the Egerton defence and Kettle scored for Forster to add a second goal. Faulty fielding by the visitors gave Fairhurst an easy try,the interval arriving with Warrington leading by two goals five tries (19 points) to Egerton nil. Turning round Warrington again demonstrated their superiority, and a brilliant breakaway ended in Daintith scoring, for Watkin to goal. Lander next put on the finishing touch to a movement initiated by a Tinkler, whilst a couple of minutes later an opportune pass by Jordan was well taken by Makin, and Egerton were further in arrears. Forster gnaled. A rapid movement on the left let in Tinkler, whilst a try by Kettle gave Forster a chance to add his fourth goal. Forster next completed the day's scoring with an unconverted try, and the Warrington record read at the close as follows :—Five goals twelve tries (46 points), to Egerton nil.

PA DGATE.

JUNIOR BAZAAR.—A junior bazaar was held in the Infants' School on Saturday, the object being to raise funds to purchase a pair of oak gates for the churchyard enlargement. For the past two years a sewing party of girls, under the superintendence of the Misses Lily and Kitty Birtles, have assembled weekly in the Church House, and the result of their labours was seen in the well laden stalls. The sale was opened by Mrs. J. r Buckton. The fancy-work stall was in charge of M. Lily Birtles, who was assisted by the Misses Heat Buckley, E. Horridge, Turner, D. and L. Rigbey, Shot and Hatton. At the refreshment stall were Miss Birtles, Mrs. Parry, Mrs. Young, Mrs. P. J. Dutton, and Misses Litton, L. Farrington, D. Parry, A. Rathbonet Ellen Critchley, R. and L. Cartwright, B. and G. Leaity and B. Young. In charge of the pound stall were Miss Kitty Birtles, B. Horridge, E. Potter, E. Malpas and D. Court, while the fish pond was in charge of Misses Marie Hey, A. Jones, D. Rigbey, E. Parry, N. Court and B. Webb. A feature of the display was a handsome doll's bed in lacquer and brass, the work of Mr. William Parry of Fearnbead, and which had been most tastefully furnished and draped by Mrs. Parry. Concerts were held and were well patronized. The artistes taking part were the Misses M. Hey, L. Hatton, A. Heath, J. Haddock and Davenport and Master Geary. The fancy dancing of Miss Haddock calls for special comment she being encored each time. The total proceeds amounted to over .£27.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible, not /ater than Thursday.

Staggering Humanity. Sir,—Since Paul Kruger, when President of the South African Republic, used that historic and prophetic phrase about " staggering humanity," I have often thought how applicable it is to some of our social evils, and to some of the sights which are often seen in our streets. 1 was forcibly reminded of it a few days ago when I saw in the streets of Warrington a boy about 14 or 15 years of age staggering under a load which was enough for a man. Noticing my inclination to speak to him, he hastened on, evidently fearing that any delay or complaint might lead to his dismissal. How many of your readers must have noticed similar cases? Cannot public opinion be brought to bear upon those who use boys like beasts of burden ? Ido not think in many cases the masters or employers are directly responsible. It is a class of thoughtless shop- men and bullies who don't care who suffer so long as the work is done at the minimum of expense and trouble to themselves. If such things are allowed to continue I fear the growing generation will grow up with many twists and distortions. The number who have been permanently injured by such heavy and unreasonabie burdens must be considerable. But this, unfortunately, is not the only way in which boys are treated with scant consideration. In works, shops, offices and warehouses some of them are little better than slaves, and Longfellow's advice to " be not like dumb driven cattle " must seem like mockery to many poor boys who are the victims of modern sweaters. At that period of their lives, after leaving school. between the ages of 14 and 18, when boys need the most help, guidance and protection, they seem to get the least. What is everybody's business is nobody's in this as in other social problems, and the remedy seems to lie in creating a centre of sympathy and activity, and in asking some section of society to be responsible. We need some organisation, say a Boys' Protection Society, to which boys could belong and to which they could apply for advice and J 1 protection when they are ill treated or oppressed whilst I trying to do their duty. Let the boy who is unjustly and harshly treated, and whose parents are unable to help him, know that, if he has done his part, Society I will at least make some little effort for him and that I someone will listen to him patiently,advise him kindly, and help him if possible. This I think would be the best and most hopeful kind of Socialism, and the truest remedy for that appalling! apathy and indifference which characterises the age. I Let Society take more interest in the welfare of the boy and then as a man he will take more interest in the I welfare of Society. The embittered Socialist who has I been hardened by injustice and adversity, says: what I did Society do for me after leaving school ? I Allowed me to be bullied, kicked about and treated like a dog, in what should have been the I happiest period of my life. Why should Ido anything I for Society. I hope and believe it is practical to form such a society as I have suggested, and in the meantime permit me to close this appeal for "our boys " with a ,Trthetic story about one of those specimens of I staggering humanity " which are seen mostly in our large cities. A boy carrying a heavy load in the streets of London fell beneath the wheels of a cab. He was carried in a dying condition to the nearest hospital. When the doctor was attending to him the boy said, "Is there any chance for me doctor ?" " Well, my lad," the doctor replied, " it's only about one chance in a hundred." "I say, doctor," the boy whispered after a painful operation, "go in like thunder on that one chance."

I wonder how many heroes such as he are staggering under heavy loads? Yours, &c., W. CHRIMES.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
679, Knutsford rd., Latchford, Jan. IS, 1903. Is Interest Robbery ?

Sir,-1 regret to see in Mr. Reeves' lettn the state- ment that he has nothing to add to his former answer. I wish his answer was as satisfactory to me as it seems to be to himself. I fear your readers will get tired of my horse and cart problem, but we get no clearer light upon the question. We saw that he who hired the horse and cart benefited by the use of this form of capital to a greater extent than the amount he paid in interest or hire, that this extra gain was the reason why he volun- tarily hired the horse and cart instead of going on with his barrow. I pointed out that the owner of the horse , and cart could have used his labour product in this or other form for his own advantage. Instead of gaining in this way he elected to take the gain in the payment from another for the use of this labour product. Where then is the wrong or robbery? Mr. Reeves' answers do not seem to me to appreciate or to meet the diffi- culty. The fact is Mr. Reeves' thoughts and my own are not on the same thing, nut- do they work on the same lines. We are approaching the question by entirely different methods. My desire first is to settle the question of capital and interest by reference to moral . considerations, and by the help of simple illustrations I in which there are no complicating factors. I want to do this as a preliminary to the application of these principles to present day social conditions. Mr. Reeves on the other hand has his mini on the social conditions of to-day and the wronc in them appears to him to be the result of the payment of interest to capital. Be therefore condemns interest as robbery, and would, without further trial, and on suspicion only, by the nniek method of lynch law hang high the interest receiver. Let me illustwe the different methods. Suppose I have a steam yacht and wish to test its steaming capacity. I should choose still water and a calm day, I should thus get the exact speed at which the yacht could travel under normal conditions. If I were to try to test the capacity of the yacht by taking it into the open sea, in the teeth of an adverse wind, against the tide and exposed to various cross currents it would be impossible for rue to estimate the capacity of the engines unless I had exact knowledge of the effect of everyone of the opposing conditions. In like manner in trying to ascertain whether the payment of interest involves robbery to labour it is best ta consider illustrations in which only the question of interest and capital is involved. In so , doing we can see exactly what effect each factor has. On the other hand by adopting Mr. Reeves' method and placing the illustrations, as he does in the latter part of his last letter„' in circumstances which confuse the issue by the introduction of various other factors, such as rent, I monopoly, wages of superintendence and profit, we are landed in a fog. By this method it is difficult to trace the influence of interest pure and simple, it is so con- fused with other factors. It seems to me therefore my plan is the better one. First settle the right or wrong of the principle of Interest by simple illustrations and then we will the better be able to trace its effect in more complex conditions. Mr. Reeves may reply that the complex conditions are the ones which exist, just as he might say that the steam yacht has to be prepared to sail against wind and tide. This is no doubt true, but just as in the case of the yacht the object was to ascertain the steaming capacity and not the effect of wind and tide, so in this matter, the object was to determine not whether labour was robbed, but whether interest was the robber. I fear that Mr. Reeves and I working on these different lines viii never come to an agreement ; this being so, I weuld suggest leaving the question, as far as this correspondence is concerned. And Ido this the more readily because I am to have the pleasure at an early date of debating this very question with the Independent Labour Party when I trust we may be able to get to closer quarters than seems possible in this correspondence. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, January 20th, 1908. LIEUTENANT-COLONEL EDELSTEN. _— Appointment older the Territorial Army Scheme. Our readers will be pleased to hear that Lieutenant- Colonel J. A. Edelsten, commanding. officer of the Warrington Volunteers, has been appointed Secretary of the West Lancashire Division Territorial Force. Members of the Volunteer Battalion in particular have reason to feel proud of the distinction which has been conferred upon the corps through its respected colonel. The appointment, of course, is a new one rendered necessary by Mr. Haldane's scheme under whieh the Volunteers cease to exist under their old name and the territorial force comes into being. The result -will be a re-modelling of the Volunteer system which -will henceforth be complete with artillery, engineers, cavalry, and medical units, all efficiently organized. Lancashire is split into two divisions —the East Lancashire Division which embraces practically the whole of the cotton district and the West Lancashire Division which covers the remainder. Each division will be responsible for the provison of 19,000 men. The headquarters of the West Lancashire Division will be at Liverpool and a great deal of the work will devolve upon Lieutenant-Colonel Edelsten as secretary. The duties, however, could not be in better hands for on the passing of the Territorial Forces Act, Colonel Edelsten was selected to take part at the War Office in the drafting at the new regula- tions and during the autumn he served on a committee under the presidency of the Secretary of State for War. He is a most enthusiastic worker in the Volunteer movement and has been connected with the Wnrrington Corps since 1887, having served as Polonel since 1003.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL

By " Ovalist."

ISHERWOOD'S BENEFIT.

" Danny " Isherwood's great thy has arrived ! To-day he will take the proceeds of the first benefit match ever given by the Warrington Club, and who shall say he has ant well-earned the honour and reward. Isherwood has been playing for the War- rington Club ever since 1895, and in that period there has been no more reliable centre threequarter or more respected player in Lancashire. He h.s played for his county too, but the bulk of his fame has been sv CI with the Warrington Club, to which he has always given loyal and whole-hearted service. I hope we shall have a fine day to-day, and that the Sparta- men of Warrington will roll up in their thousands and " pay, pay pay." Tickets for the match have sold well, and there should be a fine match and a bumper benefit.

Broughton Rangers are always a welcome side at Wilderspool, and usually give the locals a hard battle for supremacy. To-day should provide no exception, and Warrington will need to give of their best to overcome the p)werful team the Rangers have selected. On present form Warrington should certainly win, and I hope they will do it handsomely to celebrate the benefit.

The Broughton team will be as follows : —Barlow, full back ; Hardyman, C. James, Hogg, Harris, three- quarters ; S. James, Flynn, half backs ; Darlison, Mottram, Clampitt, Grainey, Winskill and Beetham, forwards.

The Warrington team will he : --Tilley. full back ; Fish, Isherwood,Dickenson and Taylor, threequarters ; Brooks and Lloyd, half backs; Thomas. A. Board- man, P. Boardman, Shngars, Heath and F. Jenkins, forwards.

WARRINGTON'S NEW WELSIIIIEN.

Warrington will soon be as Welsh as Wigan, but no lone will mind that if the quality of the players from the Land of the Leek keeps as high. This week has seen another important capture in the person of Frederick George Jenkins, of Newport, one of the reserve forwards for the Wales-England match last Saturday. Jenkins was born in South Africt, and has played football with Aberavon and Newport. He is only 21, but is spoken of as one of the speediest and smartest young forwards in Wales. Ile stands 51t. llins. high arid weighs 13st. Gibs. Warrington have been in need of another brward or two, and Jenkins should prove a valuable acquisition.

Last Saturday saw the dabut of Evan Watkins, the 'Abertillery three-quarter, with Warrington " A " team. The match was of rather a scrappy nature, ! and Watkins did not get much chance of distinguishing himself. He played a fair game, however, and was well coached by Jordan. He gave evidence of football ability, and should rapidly improve when he gets familiar with the Northern Union game.

There was a fine crowd at the " A " team match with Egerton on Saturday, and the proceeds totalled £33,0f which the visitors took half. Warrington won by 46 points to nil. Soma reckless forward charging —which ought to be checked—resulted in the -visiting full-back being injured early on.

The " A " team will have a stiff engagement to-day at Broughton, but with the strong team selected they should win. The team is : Jordan, full back ; Landor, Kenyon, and Tinkler, three-quarters ; Daintith and Cartwright, half backs ; Harmer, Jolley, Forster, Kettle, Morley, and Bradshaw, forwards.

===== 0002601_19080125_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AT SWINTON.

Swinton will have good reason to remember Warrington this season, for the Wirepullers have badly mangled the " Dons' " tail. On December 7th at Warrington the Blues had 33 points scored against them, and on Saturday Warrington ran up another 21 points. On both occasions Swinton were nilled, so they should have a good opinion of both the Warring- ton attack and defence. As a matter of fact there is only Oldham, the league leaders and the luckiest team in the universe, who have had less points scored against them than Warrington. The two ebbs stand alone in the league table, as the only ones with less than 100 points against. After beating Barrow with ease at Cavendish park, Warrington were confident of lowering the Swinton colours once more. Curiously enough the score, on successive Saturdays, was exactly the same, viz. 3 goals, 5 tries (21 points).

--- This is a standard of scoring Warrington might try to maintain. They have an outside chance of getting into one of the select four places at the top of the League, but they must beat Broughton to-day and Salford on Monday, February 3, in order to step higher. The fixture list shows only three more away matches, viz. : Leeds,Bradford and Wigan on the next three Saturdays and two or three points should be forthcoming even from these stiff engagements. , There have yet to come to Warrington : Broughton, Salford, Wigan, Bradford, Hull, Leeds, Ebbw Vale and St. Helens The local club is thus very favour- ably placed in the League, and if a determined effort is made, should stand a very fair chance of contesting the championship.

The dimplay at Swinton was all that could be desired, but in truth the opposition was terribly weak. The Swinton Lion has lost weight sadly, and presents a very poor physique Until this is remedied the promising young players in the bactc division can have little hope of improving matters.Warrington's forwards, though without Shugars, easily dominated the situa- tion, and the backs had ab.mt the easiest game of the season. Under the circumstances it would have been a disgrace if the passing had been anything else but high-class. Brooks and Jenkins were irresistible, and the three-quarters combined perfectly. Dickenson and Isherwood were both at their best, and Fish dis- tinguished himself by scoring the first four tries—a feat he has not equalled since the overwhelming of Huddersfield by 50 points last season. Seeing that he had a very bad chest, Fish's running was surprisingly good, and he quite opened the eyes of some Swinton folk who thought he was becoming a back number. Is Jack qualifying at the last minute for one of the Test matches?

It was noted that Thomas and P. Boardman dis- tinguished themselves with fine open play. Harmer distinguished himself in the opposite direction by missing an easy try when a yard from the line. He has not shown the best form of late, and in my opinion Forster should be promoted. A man who can score 20 points fnr the " A " team as he did not Saturday is too good for second-class football. With the exception of Thomas is there a single first team forward who would be likely to equal the feat in the same company? Under some clubs Forster would be considered a second Pailbury. Why will the Warrington Com- mittee never take him seriously ?

The first of the three test matches between the Northern Union and the All Blacks takes place to-day at Leeds. The NAT. team will be as follows: Taylor (Hull), Leytham and Jenkins (Wigan) ; Llewellyn (Oldham) and Eccles (Halifax) ; Jolley (Runcorn) and J. Thomas (Wigan) ; L. Smith (Oldham), I). Jones (Merthyr), Robinson (Halitax), Wilson (Hunslet), Warwick (Salford), and Ruddick (Broughton).

in defiance of the law. If the director said they would not be within their legal rights in granting an exemp tion, they stood by the law. The Committee reserved the right to take into consideration special circumstances which warranted exemptions, in accordance with the intention and spirit of the law. If the resolution were referred back it would only meet with the same fate,so the Committee would only be wasting time. Rather than reconsider the matter he would resign his seat, if he was not to allow his sympathies to weigh in extra- ordinary cases, his position would be extremely painful and, in fact, intolerable, and he could not stand it. The Committee would be foolish in parting with the authority which enabled them to deal with extreme cases.

Mr. KINGDON said the point or issue was whether the Committee had any discretionary power.in the direction indicated by Councillor Neild.

Mr. KonEras said his opinion was that the Committee had no discretion at all. Their duty was so very clearly defined that they had no option if a child between the ages of 13 and 14 was shown to the satisfaction of the authority to be beneficially employed, but to grant an exemption under certain conditions plainly set forth. Income and position did not effect the question. The child of the biggest blackguard could demand exemption if employed to the satisfaction of the Committee. Judging from what had been said the Committee wanted the discretion which they had been exercising in defiance of the restrictions Contained in the bye-laws. He entirely agreed that the time had come when the school age should be raised. If the Committee bad granted exemptions on other grounds than those set forth in the bye-laws and had therefore acted Illegally, the general Committee had no right to sanction such illegality. The DIRECTOR said that on several occasions the School Attendance Committee had been told by him- self or his assistant that the exemption they were granting was Illegal. He thought the members of the Committee would bear that out.

Councillor BALL said the statement had been made in his presence many times. The committee, he con- tinued, had interpreted beneficial employment as being home employment for the assistance of the mother with a young and struggling family, but such exemptions were strictly illegal and Mr. Murray had pointed it out on several occasions. He (Councillor Ball) at the last committee meeting proposed that it should be impossibe for a child who had not passed the seventh standard to leave school unless it was 14 years of age, and that the discretionary power should be dispensed with. It was an ironical commentary on our exist- ing social order that it should be left to a sub-committee of the Education Committee to deal with poverty and distress when their special object was to see that the child had a proper education, and he felt that his attitude was in accord with the general views expressed by trade union and labour forces. He was surprised that the local Trades Council was not prepared to grant power to its representative to support his resolution. He could only assume that in labour politics, be was somewhat ahead of the members locally. (Laughter.) In 1904 the percentage of exemptions was 5-9 ; in 1905 5.8 ; in 1906 78 ; and 1907 73. That seemed to show that the Com- mittee were being more lenient than they ought to be in the granting of exemptions. Many cases which came before them appealed to his own sentiment and heart, but he had always felt that in the long run it was in the best interests of the family and the children that for one year longer the child should be compelled to go to school. Under their recently-conferred powers for the feeding of school children, they had the opportunity of relieving some of the poverty of the most painful nature. Therefore they should see that the age limit was raised and adhered to. In his resolutions his only supporter was Father Wilson. the Rev. Father WILSON said he was extremely sur- prised that Councillor Ball's proposal was opposed by those whom he (Pother Wilson) thought would be the I first to support it. He understood their policy was to discourage child labour for the very reason that it reflected hardship on adults who were out of work and he hold that, though there might be individual cases of temporary hardship, it would be very much for the general good of the town if the resolution abolishing what was called the " dunce " certificate had been carried. He did not think it was fair to the Committee that they should have the discretionary power. The School Attendance Committee was not the authority to deal with cases of hardship. Their duty was to administer the law. Mr. JOLLEY said that when he read the School Attendance Committee's resolution he wondered whether the Committee was composed of fossil Tories and whether we were living in the 20th ' century or not. (Laughter.) When he came to look at the personnel of the Committee he found it would not be open to the just accusation of consist- ing largely of Conservative members and he felt a little relieved on the score. If the mambori could not be described as fossil Tories he was afraid some of them must be almost prehistoric fogeys,at any rate.( Laughter.) qr. Jolley strongly supported the suggested raising of the school age aud the abolition of any discretionary power, and maintained that if the School Attendance Committee deliberately set at defiance the wishes of the I full committee, the full committee had means of making refractory committees carry out what was or ought to be the deliberate conviction of anyone who came there with the interests of education at heart. Mr. Upson had rendered a public service by moving the amendment. They could not have progress without the cases of hardship. Many of the evils alluded to were due to moral causes and some to economic causes. It was, however, no part of the committee's duty to shirk their I responsibilities in regard to the special purpose for which they were called into existence. Mr. G. WALKEa said the Committee had interpreted beneficial employment very much in favour of distressed parents. Where he had thought it would be to the advantage of a household that a child should be freed from attendance at school, he had willingly agreed to it, because he believed that something to eat was often of more use than education. He did not agree that deserving parents should be pauperised in order to obtain relief. It was imposhible to listen to the harrow- ing tales of some of the people who appeared before them without allowing them to have some weight. Nlr. BRADSHAW suggested that Mr. Jolley should be transferred to the School Attendance Committee as he appeared to be possessed of all the intelligence or at least the committees with which he was connected had all the intelligence. (Laughter.) If he joined the School attendance Committee he would perhaps not hear as much "tittle-tattle"as on some other bodies with which he was connected and the committee did not even want a Local Government Board Inspector to correct it. (Laughter.) The Rev. JAMES DAVIES suggested that the granting bwithe eibuyi vt leo oi nt sitni t vu at no oe woulde Is e d oo ff ef rxeeemspothi oo lnasr could ho iupl sd wb eh idoihs to the money which the children could earn. Councillor NIELD said he had been under a misappre- hension. It they were not acting within their legal rights, he withdrew all that he had said and was willing that the matter should be referred back. He had been absolutely misled. Alderman ELLISON said the extreme case mentioned by Mr. Upson was re-considered and the parents were brought to book. BAND OF HOPE UNION.

Protest Against Proposed Licensing Transfer.

A large and representative social gathering of Band of Hope workers, was held in the %Vintner Leigh Caf4, Sankey-street, on Thursday evening under the presidency of Mrs. James Dodds (president of the Union), who held a reception from 7.30 to 8.0. The proceedings opened with prayer by the Rev. C. Harvey- Cook. In her presidential address Mrs. Dodds, who was enthusiastically received, expressed the great pleasure it afforded her to meet such a large assembly of Band of Hope workers, and spoke of the increasing need for further effort to extend the move- ment and counteract the efforts the brewers and publicans were making to capture the town. She also appealed to all present to do a little more for the temperance c 'use and more especially amongst the rising generation who would be the men and women of the future. She also expressed her deep regret that the hon. secretary (Mr. F. Gaudy), was unable to be present owing to family affliction and also referred to the illness of their late president (Mr. R. A. Naylor) and expressed the hope that he would speedily be restored to health and strength, and also that Mr. Gaudy might soon be able to again join in the work of the Union which he had so much at heart. (Hear, hear.) Au interesting and instructive address was delivered by Mr. Edwards, of Owen's College, Manchester, Lecturer of the Lancashire and Cheshire B. H. Union, in which he spoke of the many advantages of total abstinence ; dealing principally with the effects of alcohol on the mental powers. On the proposition or the Rev. C. HARVEY COOK, seconded by Mr. FERNLEY a heists, Vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Eiwarde, and on the Proposition of Rev.J.S.HuGHEs seconded by Mr.T.Dar.r: a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. D )(ids for her kindness in presiding. Mr. EDWARDS and Mrs. DODDS responded, after which refreshments were served and the rest of the evenint was devoted to gi nes in which the young people entered most heartily. During the evening a pe:ition calling upon the Government to include in its new Licensing Bill a clause to prohibit children under It; years of age being taken into public-houses Pres extensively signed. The following resolution was proposed by Mrs. Dodds : " That this meeting of the Warrington Bind of Hope workers desires to enter its emphatic protest against the proposed transfer of the Bridge Inn licence to the Gorsey-lame district arid whilst pointing out that the evil will be greatly aggravated owing to the proximity of the Bearnont Council School, expresses the hope that the transfer will not be granted." The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS said that as a Band of Hope Union they were directly interested in child life. That was a simple and significant reason for his cordially seconding the resolution. The resolution was unanimously passed.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" The Little areadwinner.."

This is a simple,homeli and touching drama and,as presented by J. A. Campbell's powerful company, has constituted a strong attraction at the Royal Court Theatre this week. Mr. Campbell is the author of the piece and the fact that the production is under his supervision is a guarantee of its completeness. The play is well written and makes a powerful appeal io the sympathies of the audience. Lord William Do rrington, a Quaker of the strictest type, has a win- some grand-daughter, Margaret Daventry, who is engaged to Richard Lawrence, his adopted son. When the play opens Richard has been giving Lord William some anxiety by his wildness, and Charles Prior,his lordship's private secretary,a cringing Uriah Heep-like sort of character, who has determined to enrich himself at his master's expense, poisons the stern old Quaker's mind by false charges against the wayward Richard. Lird William's pride in the family honour receives such a shock that he turns Richard from home but Niargaret, confident oE his innocence, bravely declares her intention of accompanying her lover. Eleven years elapse and Richard arid his wife, robbed by the designing secretary of the allowance which Lord William had arranged to send, are living in direst poverty. Their one comfort is their daughter, little -Meg, " the bread-winner " who supplements the scanty earnings of her father by singing in the streets. She is a little fairy in a scene of utter desolation and helps to cheer the home on many a dark day: How a reconciliation is at length affected between Lord William and the much- wronged couple, despite the machinations of Prior, is seen in the rest of the play which is not withaut its exciting and laughable sides. The "little bread- winner " of courser plays her part in the happy con- summation for she speedily captures the affections of Lord William and is the popular heroine of a delight- ful play. In the title role little Marjorie Lawrence ' does admirably and gives every promise of a success- ful career. She is not only a pleasing actress but can sing and dance cleverly, and her sweet rendering of the pathetic song " Daddy " is one of the most " taking " features. Mr. H. B. Peppin has made a • good study of the part of Lord William Dorrington which could hardly be better pourtrayed. Mr. Alfred • Maurice as the confidential secretary displays talent of a high order, the alternating moods of pious hypocrisy and open villainy being strikingly depicted. Mr. Louis Lingard gives a fine and vig woes version of Richard Lawrence and Miss Janet Warden is a charming and devoted t.irgaret Da ventry. Mr.Geo.Brentwo d infuses a great deal of buoyant humour into the role of Charles Prior, and Miss Lissa Young makes a decided " hit " as Bobby Trott.. All the other parts are in capable ' hands.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. The long looked-for visit of Messrs. Dottridge and Longden's pantomime "Aladdin" which has been an enormous success in Liverpool, is announced for next week. The many sucesses of the above firm are well known to playgoers, their dramstic productions including " King of Crime," " A City of Sin," " The Village Blacksmith," "In the Shadow of Night," " Man's Enemy," "The Midnight Mail," &c. For over twenty years they have been catering for the public and the pantomine promises to enhance their high reputation. The company will include Miss Fanny Wright as " Aladdin" and Mr. Picton Roxborough as " The Emperor." There will be numerous speciality turns, and the staying and dressing will be on an elaborate scale. A matinee will be held on Saturday afternoon at 2.30.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE. THE GRANTING OF EXEMPTIONS. School Attendance Committee Criticised.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee was held at the Town Hall on Monday even- ing, Councillor J. Fulton-Smith presiding. There were also present :—Aldermen Tionion, Bennett, Evans and Ellison, Councillors Barton, Evans, Dickenson, Singleton, Nield, Steel, Ball. Revs. Canon Morley Stevenson, Father N. Wilson and John Yonge, Miss Williams, Messrs. H. Roberts, A. J. Jolley, C. S. Burgess, J. Upson, C. E. Parker, R. T. Fairclough, E. Plinston, T. P. Bradshaw, EL Kingdon, G. Walker, C. H. Unsworth, J. Moore Murray (director of education), and J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer). A letter was read from Mrs. James Fairclough acknowledging the committee's vote of condolence on the death of her husband. OFFICIALS' SALARIES.

The Finance Committee recommended the following increases of salary from April Ist :—Mr. A. S. Elliot, stores clerk, from £143 10s. to £l5O ; Mr. I. C. Pharoah, higher education clerk, from £94 to £lOO 103. ; Mr. R. Price, statistical clerk, from £5B 10s. to £65 ; Miss M. E. Austin, typist, from £5B 10s. to £65 ; Mr. P. W. Smith, committee and minute clerk,from £39 to £45 10s.

Mr. JOLLEY explained that the increases were of a routine character, and in accordance with the scale for the office.

The minutes were approved. TEACHERS' SALARIES.

The School Nianagement Committee submitted the annual list of increases of sAlaries of elementary school teachers' the total amount of advances being £1,078. Canon STENENSON in moving the adoption of the minutes said he regretted the list of increases was a longer one than that moved by Mr. Jolley—(laughter) —but the same remark applied to it—it was simply according to scale. BAPTISTS AND THE BOLTON SCHOOL. . . _ ' '

At a meeting of the school Management Committee, letters were read from the Minister and Secretary of the Latchford Baptist Church as to the constitution of the body of Managers appointed for the new Bolton Council school, and directions were given as to the , reply to be sent thereto. The Rev. Canon STEVENSON said the letters were to the effect that the great proportion of the managers were attached to one particular denomination and that was felt to be a grievance. The committee had never made any inquiry,in appointing managers,as to their religious opinions and he ventured to think it would be a great mistake if they did. He personally was uot cognisant of the religious denomination to which some of the gentlemen belonged until the committee received the illuminating letter which shed light upon it. What they wanted to get was not persons of one particular denomination or another,but persons who were interested in the neighbourhood where the school was situated, who would give the necessary time, and who had some experience in the management of schools and took a real interest in the school itself. He hoped the com- mittee would take a broad view of the question and not be led into what he must think to be the somewhat narrow path which the Latchford letter had placed before then).

BATHS ACCO AIM° DATION. A PLEA. FOR THE GIRLS. Canon STEVENSON said the Committee regretted very much that the Baths Committee were unable at present to extend to the girls the facilities which had been afforded with such good results to the boys. fhey had hoped that it might be possble to arrange, if only once or twice in a week, that the ladies' bath might be placed at the disposal of the girls and they regretted it could not be so.However,there was hope of an extension of the baths in due course and when that came about they hoped they would be able to do something for the girls. He hoped the extension would take place sooner than the Warrington Bridge improvement (Laughter.) Ever since he came to Warrington twenty-ilve years ago the bridge was either going to be widened or a new bridge was going to be made. He still lived in hopes of seeing that done, but it required a certain amount of faith now. (Laughter.) He trusted the extension of the baths would not be so long I delayed, for he felt sure public opinion was ripening on the subject and if they did not do something for the girls soon they would be turning them into suffragettes. (Laughter.) Mr. UPsoNsaid many members of the committee were of opinion that something might be done at the present time. It WAS possible they might hive to wait until they were dead find forgogen before any extension of the baths took place. (Applause.) Thev thought that even with the present cramped accommodation some facilities might be afforded LANCASHIRE TRAINING COLLEGES. Canon STEVENSON explained that the School Manage- ment Cinamittee recommended that repnesentatives be appointed to attend a conference at the invitation of the Lancashire County f" ninon, who had asked whether Warrington would be to co-operate with other authorities in the county to provide one or more training colleges. The question, he said, was not so simple as it appeared at first sight. He would not go into the matter, because it was in its first stage, but there could be no harm in attending the conference, sad hearing what other people had to say. The whole of the School Management Committee's minutes were unanimously approved.

THE GRANTING; c..[ EXENIPTIONS. HAS THE CONINIITTEE EXCEEDED ITS POWERS?

At a meeting of the School Attendance Committee, notice of motion having been given to amend the bye- laws dealing with school attendance (made under the various Education Acts) by the omission of Clause 5 (b), viz. :—" A child between 13 and 14 years of age, shown to the satisfaction of the Local Authority to be beneficially employed, shall not be required to attend school, if such child has obtained a certificate that it has made 350 attendances, after five years of age, in not inure than two schools in each year for five years, whether consecutive or not."—The matter was carefully considered, and the motion was put to the vote, two voting for and seven against it. The motion being lost, it was resolved, that a reply be sent to the communication received from the Warrington Head Teachers' Association upoa this subject, to the effect that this Committee is not pre- pared to recommend the Education Coffmittee to withdraw the discretionary power contained in the bye-laws, and that they will continue to carry out the bye-laws as they at present exist. Alderman ELLISON in moving the adoption of the Committee's minutes said they were still face to face with the question of accommodation which had been before the Committee since 1809. There was now almost a threat on the part of the Board of Education to reduce the grant, and he hoped steps would be taken to carry out the necessary alterations at the school. Councillor BARTON seconded. Mr. UesoN moved that the resolution with regard to the granting of exemptions be not approved. He did not charge all the members of the committee with indifference on that vital question but he thought the majority wanted a little light betting into their minds with regard to it. The present byelaws provided that if a child at the age of 13 could produce a certificate showing it had made 350 attendances for five years, it °quid be exempted from further attendance at school. Every educational authority and every educationist,had felt for a long time that the mininram was a very low one and that the ideal condition would be to keep the child in school until it was 14. All Labour Associations, Trades Councils and Trades Conferences had pleaded for the raising of the school age and many authorities had moved in the matter, thus giving the scholars in those towns a better start in life than Warrington I children had. The Head Teachers' Association quarrelled with some members of the Committee because they had not applied the existing byelaws as they stood. They had adopted a certain method of special exemption by which they allowed children to leave school who had not complied with the statutory obligations of the byelaws. That was a somewhat serious thing to say, hilt it was a fact. " Our fathers" continued Mr. Up 4011" fought against the autocracy of Kings. We defeated long since the oligarchy of the nobles. It has remained for the 20th century to pro- dnce a body of gentlemen who are above the law. They sit to administer the law and they themselves break the law." (Laughter.) He could give many startling instances of what the Committee had seen fit to do. The Committee had no power to partially exempt a child from attendanoe except for half-time under the Factory Acts and they had no power to say a child could leave school at the age of 13 if it could not pro- duce a certificate of reviler attendance. Although the comments of the head teachers were invited as to the wisdom or otherwise of granting an exemption in every Case, very little notice was taken of the remarks by the Committee. He had protested again and again against the granting of exemptions where there had been no need for them. In one case an exemption was granted on the ground of poverty when the father was earning £3 or .C 4 a week and had actually bought tne boy us bicycle and a football outfit. Mr. JOLLEY seconded the amendment. Mr. PLINSTON strongly supported the Committee's action, pointing out that the strictest inquiries were made when the parents appeared before the Committee, and it was impossible to ignore the poverty-stricken conditions of many who applied for exemptions. In those cases it was the bounden duty of the Committee to exercise their discretionary power. Mr. Upson had selected a black case from his point of view, but there were scores, almost hundreds of others which were just the opposite, the father being in receipt of about 17s. 10d. a week, and having to maintain a family. In many instances the applications for exemptions were supported by the head teachers on the ground that the child was required to help to contribute to the earnings of the family. Councillor 'NIELD said he did not accept the state. ment that the Committee had acted on any occasion illegally. If they had, their appeal to the Di rector had met with a response which could only be interpreted as lending them astray, because he did not remember an Instance in which they had deliberately set themselves

INFIRMARY BALL.

BRILLIANT FUNCTION AT THE

PARR HALL.

A brilliant and highly successful function was the annual ball in aid of the Warrington Infirmary which was held at the Parr Hall on Wednesday evening. This year a new departure was made in engaging the Parr Hall and it is believed that the financial result will fully justify the change. Over 200 tickets were sold and the attendance numbered 190. The hall had been very artistically decorated by Mr. Ralph Johnson and the effect was most pleasing. Flowers had been tastefully arranged by Mrs. Henry G-reenall and Mrs. W. P. Rylands and the introduction of plants I lent by Lady Greenall, Mrs. Henry Greenall and Mrs. Parr further enhanced the beauty of the scene. The provision of a special narquet floor added much to the pleasure of the dancers. Admirable music was discoursed by Ill's band and dancing was kept up with zest until 2.30 a.m. The duties of stewards were ably discharged by Dr. E. E. Bowden. Major A. F. Bundock, Mr. Henry Greenall, Mr. James Hepherd, Mr. W. Peter Rylands, Major C.J. Steavenson, and Captain A. de V. Willoughby-Osborne. The catering was in the hands of the Winnaarleigh Cafe Co. The public were admitted to the galleries to hear the music and watch the dancing at a charge of one shilling each, but com- paratively few availed themselves of the opportunity. Praise is due to the following committee which was responsible for the admirable arrangements : President, Mrs. Pickmere ; the Mayoress (Mrs. Smethurst), Lady Greenall, Mrs. Airey, Mrs. Armitage, Mrs. Blair, Mrs. Broadbent, Mrs. J. • CHRONIC WHOOPING COUGH to convert one of their houses in Bewsey-road into a Cured after all else Failed, by shop RS soon as they could obtain possession. That, they hoped, would greatly relieve the trade in VENO'S LIGHTNING COUGH CURE. Winwick-street. The arrangements contemplated for converting the Hall at Winwick-street into a central distributing warehouse had been completed, and tenders received and accepted for new offices in Cairo-street, which were well in hand, and they hoped to have them shortly opon and the present office premises available for business extensions. They had some new light vans on order' and as soon es they were ready they hoped to increase their bread deliveries in the town. Their trade during the quarter was £45,957 13s. 10W., an increise of £1,543 17s. 2;d. over the corresponding quarter of last year. The membership at the close of the year was 8,070. The profits, after meeting all expenses, would allow of a dividend of 3s, in the £ on members' pur- chases and a small balsnce to carry forward to next quarter. The CHAIRMAN stated that the new shop in Marsh- street would be opened next Thursday. The report end balance sheet were adopted on the proposition of the CHAIRMAN seconded by Mr. MAYBORY. MR. R. A. NAYLOR. -- Reported Sinking. The grave news reached us last night from our Langford correspondent that Mr. rt. A. Netylor, of Langford Park, Somerset, was gradually sinking, and no hopes could be entertained of his recovery. fie passed a fairly quiet night on Thursday, but his strength was not maintained yesterday.
